3015. LOUISA ACOX was again indicted for stealing, on the 6th of October, I thermometer and frame and case, value 15 s., the goods of Spencer Joshua Alwyne, Marquis of Northampton.
DAVID DAVIS . I am under butler to Spencer Joshua Alwyne, Marquis of Northampton. The prisoner lived there as house-maid about twelve months ago—I missed several things, and, amongst the rest, a thermometer and frame and case, from the study—the case is here, but the frame and
the thermometer are gone—I believe this case to be the Marquis's, but I could not swear to it—I missed it about a month after the prisoner left.
JURY. Q. Was this perfect the last time you saw it? A. Yes—this is not the crest of the Marquis which is on it.
GEORGE ROGERS . I am a police-officer. I took the prisoner—I searched her room and box, and found this frame.
Prisoner's Defence. It was among some dust. It was throwing about some time in our room; I did not know it was of any consequence.
GUILTY . Aged 38.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18421024-3016]
3016. MARIA HARRISON was indicted for stealing, on the 24th of October, I sovereign, 3 half-crowns, 3 shillings, 2 sixpences, and I groat, the monies of Thomas Henwood, from his person.
THOMAS HENWOOD . I live in Quebec-street, St. Marylebone, and am a carpenter. On the 24th of October I went to a public-house, where I staid till a quarter past twelve o'clock at night—the prisoner was there—we drank together, and then left—the prisoner came away with my nephew sod me—I told my nephew not to leave me—we turned down Portmansquare and down Oxford-street—I was the worse for liquor—I then went to an archway, for a certain purpose, leaving my nephew and the prisoner standing on the pavement—when I came back she was gone—my nephew said, "Have you got your watch?"—I said, "Yes"—I then missed my money—I lost about 1 l. 12 s.—I know I had a sovereign and three half-crowns, a shilling, and some sixpences, and a fourpenny piece in my pocket—I sent Ruddy to find where she lived—he brought her to me next evening, but nothing was found on her.
GEORGE SMITH . I am the prosecutor's nephew. As he was walking along, I did not see the prisoner put her hand in his pocket, but she was rummaging about his trowsers.
WILLIAM RUDDY . I went to the Three Tuns—I saw the prosecutor and the prisoner in company there—we came out at a quarter after twelve o'clock, and I wished him good night—next morning he sent to me to say she had robbed him—I found her, and told her I had come about Mr. Henwood's case, and that she had robbed him of 1 l. 12 l. or 1 l. 14 s.—she said she had not taken so much, only a sovereign—I asked if she had any money left—she said no, she had bought a shawl and various things with it.
Prisoner. He gave me a sovereign, and that I bought the shawl with.
THOMAS HENWOOD re-examined. On my oath, I did not give it her—I was the worse for liquor—my little boy sat facing me all the time—she asked me to give her a pint of beer, and, to the best of my knowledge, I gave her a pint of half-and-half, and then I gave her something to eat.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18421024-3017]
3017. HENRY SMITH was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of October, 1 cap, value 5 s.; and 1 handkerchief, value 3 s.; the goods of Stephen Jarman, in a vessel in a port of entry and discharge.
STEPHEN JARMAN . I am a seaman on board the barque Emma, which was lying in the St. katherine's Docks. On the 24th of October the prisoner was on board—I saw him closing the lid of my chest—I saw a handkerchief lying on the cabin floor—I asked what he had been doing at the chest—he said to get some bread—I looked, and missed the cap and handkerchief
—I saw something under his trowsers, gave him in charge, and the cap was found on him—this is my cap and handkerchief—he had'no business with them, nor at my box—he did not belong to the vessel.
Prisoner. A man was coming up the forecastle—I asked him to give me something to eat—he said I might go down and get something—I went down and helped myself to a bit of bread; the cap and handkerchief laid in one of the bed places.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18421024-3018]
3018. JOHN WOOD was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of October, 1 pair of trowsers, value 1 l., the goods of John Taylor and another.
THOMAS TAYLOR . I am in partnership with John Taylor, of Tottenham Court-road. About a quarter before eight o'clock, on the night of the 22nd of October, I saw the prisoner take a pair of trowsers off the rail—I followed—he was brought back and given into custody—the trowsers were picked up about two doors off, and brought back to the shop—I did not see the prisoner throw them down, but I saw him take them.
Cross-examined by MR. ESPINASSE. Q. Have you any other Dime? A. No—these trowsers hung on a rail outside the shop—when he took them my face was towards him.
WILLIAM GILBERT ( police-constable E 136.) I took the pritouer—he said if I would let him go he would never do the like again. The prisoner received a good character, and a witness engaged to employ him.
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Nine Days.
[trialAccount t18421024-3019]
3019. JOHN ARMSTRONG was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of October, 2 spoons, value 1 l., the goods of Benjamin Baker.
FREDERICK BAKER . I live with my father, benjamin Baker, at Stntford. We had two silver spoons safe on the 14th of October—one was in the kitchen, and the other lying in the yard, by the back-door—we missed them—this produced is one of them—I saw the prisoner on our premises the day before—my brother was there that day, and we missed him as well as the spoons.
Cross-examined. Q. One of the spoons had been taken out of the kitchen? A. Yes—I do not know who by—I saw the spoons between four and six o'clock on Friday, the 14th, and missed them the next day—the one that was lying at the back-door was not seen again till I found it at the silversmith's—my brother has been to sea.
COURT. Q. Have you charged your brother with stealing these? Yes—the prisoner was a friend of his.
WILLIAM VELLENWITH . I am shopman to Mr. Midrileton; he lives in the Commercial-road. I bought this spoon of a young man—I cannot say that it was the prisoner.
ROBERT HARRILE . I live in the Commercial-road. I bought a silver table-spoon, similar to this one, of the prisoner.
JOHN CAVERLEY ( police-constable K 203.) I took the prisoner on the Bow-road—be passed—I called him by his name—my brother officer told him he was charged with robbing Mr. Baker of two spoons—he said it was the first he had heard of it.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18421024-3020]
3020. ROBERT SHORE was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of September, 1 table, value 10 s., the goods of Elizabeth Robinson.
ELIZABETH ROBINSON . I live in Corporation-row, Clerkenwell—I hired a room of Mrs. Burgess in August last, and furnished it—I was to hare been married to the prisoner next Christmas—I had a table in the room, a bed, and four blankets—I did not authorize him to take any of them away.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. YOU hired the room to deposit the furniture? A. Yes—the prisoner helped me to take the bed and pillow, but not the table—he has married my sister instead of me—I had only been to Mrs. Burgess with him once about these things—I have had no difference with him—I am not on bad terms with my sister—I have several times been in a house in Brunswick-street, St. John's-street-road—my sister lodged there—I do not know Fielding—I never was there when any conversation took place between me and the prisoner—I swear I never said anything to him about taking these goods to another place—he borrowed a sovereign of me, and he said he could make five or ten shillings—it was not got from me on account of these goods—the banns were put up for our marriage by Fielding—there was no difference with the prisoner and me after that.
MARY ANN BURGESS . I am the wife of Joseph Burgess—the prisoner came to me on the Saturday, and brought me the week's rent, and said Mrs. Robinson had sent it—the table was in my parlour, and the prisoner took it away—he also took the bed and bedding—he said Miss Robinson had sent him for it.
MR. HORRY called,
SAMUEL FIELDING . I live in Brunswick-street, St. John's-street-road—I know the prisoner and the prosecutrix—she has frequently come to my house—I remember her being in my room with the prisoner—some conversation took place about this furniture, and she authorised him to take the things from where they were deposited, and put them where he thought proper, till they were married—I pot up the banns for their marriage—it was after that that the goods were moved.
COURT. Q. When was it that conversation took place? A, I think about ten weeks ago—I cannot state the day.
ELIZABETH ROBINSON re-examned. When he was going to marry me he took the goods for me, with the exception of the table—I never authorised him to take the table—he married my sister about a fortnight after taking the goods.
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Transported for Seven Years.
(There was another indictment against the prisoner.)
[trialAccount t18421024-3021]
3021. JOSEPH FOOKES was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. ESPINASSE conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN GRAY . I keep the White Hart in the Mile End-road—the prisoner was my barman about four months—he received money from my different customers—when he received it he ought to have put it in the till, or on the hob—on the 8th of October he did not account to me for 1 s. 4 d. for gin, that had been sold—I examined my till that day—I found no sovereign in it—he was not authorised to put any money into his pocket.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q, It was not usual to account for the sale of particular portions of liquor? A. Certainly not—I expected, if anything was sold, that the money should be put into the till—there generally is enough money in the till to give change—I had told him there was a great deficiency in beer and gin—the policeman saw him at half-past eleven—I had seen the till three or four times in the course of the half hour, for I was putting marked money in—I cannot tell how much was in the till or on the hob at half-past eleven—I was clearing the till as fast as possible.
MR. ESPINASSE. Q. Can you say, on the 8th of October, whether there was sufficient in the till or on the hob to give change for the sorereign? A. I should say there was.
EDWARD WANDERER TOWNSON ( police-constable K 143.) I was at Mr. Gray's at half-past eleven in the morning—I saw the prisoner serving at the bar—I saw Mrs. Thompson go in—she called for a pint of gin—the prioner served her—she gave him a sovereign—he put it into his waistcoatpocket, took some change from his pocket, and gave her—I watched him for twenty minutes after the woman went away, to see if he appropriated it to the till—he did not—Thompson came back, and he gave her a 6 d. which he took from the till—I took him into custody, and cautioned him against saying anything—I asked where he got the money—he said, from Mr. Gray—Mr. Gray gave him the shilling—he did not say anything about this—another officer found a sovereign.
ANN THOMPSON . On the 8th of October I went to Mr. Gray's for a pint of gin—it came to 1 s. 4 d.—the prisoner served me—I gave him a sovereign—he put it into his left hand trowsers' pocket—he gave me one half sovereign, and the rest in silver, out of his pocket—he gave me 6 d. short at first, and then he gave it me out of the till.
HENRY JOHN PARKER ( police-constable K 10.) I asked the prisoner what money he had about him—he said "A sovereign, two shillings, and two sixpences."
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Nine Months.
(There were two other indictments against the prisoner.)

3022. ELIZA WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of October, 2lbs. 10oz. weight of ham, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of John Loveridge.
ELIZABETH JEFFRIES . I live in Shouldham-street. On Wednesday afternoon, the 19th of October, I saw the prisoner take a piece of ham from the prosecutor's shop.
CHARLOTTE GILES . The prisoner came to my room that day, and asked me to let her stop for a few minutes, she would make me a recompense—I pushed her down stairs, and the prosecutor stood there with the ham in his hand.
RICHARD LOVERIDGE . I am the son of John Loveridge. I followed the prisoner up to this place, and saw her throw this ham away—I stood till she came out, and I took her—it was my father's.
Prisoner's Defence. Distress caused me to do it.
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Confined Three Months.

Prisoners Defence. They gave me the cold meat.

JAMES BRANNAN ( police-constable N 69.) 3027. WILLIAM JORDAN was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of October, 2 shillings, the monies of George Webb, his master.
GEORGE WEBB. I am the prisoner's master. On the 19th of October, I marked two shillings, and put them into the till at a quarter before eight o'clock in the evening—soon after, Mrs. Zimmerman's servant came in for some butter—I went to the till soon after, and found the two shillings gone—the policeman searched the prisoner, and found one in his pocket—I do not know what has become of the other.
Cross-examined by MR. PATNE. Q. YOU were only five minutes absent? A. Yes—the prisoner had been only five weeks in my service.
JOHN FREDERICK ZIMMERMAN . I went to the prosecutor's shop—I did not receive any shilling from the prisoner—this other shilling has my mark on it, which Gregory, my man, paid there.
Cross-examined. Q. What is the mark on this other shilling? A. A mark under the chin with the point of a nail—it enables me to swear to it—I gave it to Joseph Gregory, my shopman, from half-past seven till a quarter to eight o'clock.
WILLIAM TILLIER ( police-constable C 28.) I was called and searched the prisoner—I found two shillings on him, which I now produce.
JOSEPH GREGORY . I paid this shilling to the prisoner.
Cross-examined. Q. You did not see what was done with it? A. I did not.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 21.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Four Months,

3029. JOHN WILSON was indicted for a misdemeanor.
JOHN TANNER . I keep an outfitter's shop in the Minories. The prisoner came and represented himself to keep a tailor's and outfitter's shop—he said he had to get a chest of drawers and a washing-stand for Captain Clark—that he lived at No. 6, Lincoln's Inn-fields, and we were to make the drawers—he said he was making clothes for Captain Clark—I said I should like to see Captain Clark, in case he would like any alteration—he came again in a day or two, with a drawing of the drawers and a signature—I got on with them, and he ordered me to send them a week afterwards—believing that he had an order from Captain Clark, I sent these things—I have inquired, and found the prisoner keeps a cabinet-maker's shop in Lincoln's Inn-fields, not a tailor's and outfitter's—I have not found Captain Clark.
WILLIAM WORFORD . I am a blacksmith, living at Croydon. I was employed by the prosecutor to carry the drawers on the 14th of September to No. 6, Lincoln's Inn-fields—it was a lawyer's chambers—I made Inquiries, and could find no such person—I returned with the drawers, and on the 16th I went there, and the prosecutor said, "I have sent two boys with the drawers; I want you to go and see them delivered safe"—I went, and saw one of the boys with a truck—the prisoner was pushing behind—he said, "How is it you did not bring them up last Wednesday?"—I said, "I was up here, but could not find you"—he said, "It is up here, and it was No. 6, Bear-yard—there was no shop at No. 6, Lincoln's Inn-fields.
WILLIAM PRIEST . I live in the Strand. The prisoner came and asked if I purchased any articles—he said he was a cabinet-maker—I and I did, but did not want any—he called again, and said he had an article at the house in Bear-yard, and I bought these drawers of him for 5 l.
Prisoner's Defence. I had orders from Captain Clark for a chest of drawers; Mr. Tanner sent them home; they were not made to order, and the gentleman would not have them.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18421024-3030]
3030. PATRICK MARD was indicted for a misdemeanor.
THOMAS HANDLEY . I am foreman to George Bird, a builder, in Edgeware-road—the prisoner was employed on the sewers there. On 22nd of September I discharged him, and gave him a note for 11 s. 6 d.—on the
Saturday morning following he came to the works, and asked me to accommodate him, because it was nearly two miles to go to my employers—I said I would pay him the money, and asked for the note that I gave him—he said he had lost it—I paid him 7 s. 6 d. and 4 s.—he went away with it—this is the note I gave him.
FRANCIS WATSON . I am clerk to George Bird. On the morning of the 24th of September the prisoner came and produced this note—I paid him three half-crowns, and four shillings, which was the amount of his wages—if he had not brought me that note I should not have paid him.
Prisoner's Defence. In January last Handley borrowed 12 s. of me; he never paid me; I was afraid to ask him, for fear be should discharge me. On the 23rd he discharged me; I asked him for the 12 s.; he did not pay me, but gave me a note for 10 s. I went again, and he wrote me an order for 11 s. 6 d.
GUILTY Aged 40.— Confined Four Months.

WILLIAM BELL was indicted for stealing, on the 25th of October, 2 pairs of pantaloons, value 1 l.; I shirt, value 3 s.; 2 pairs of drawers, value 4 s.; 2 pairs of stockings, value 3 s.; 5 printed books, value 2 s.; I bag, value 1 s. 6 d.; 2 waistcoats, value 4 s.; 3 shirt-fronts, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 1 pair of gloves, value 1 s.; the goods of James Dowries, in a certain vessel, in a port of entry and discharge.

[trialAccount t18421024-3037]
3037. PETER JAMES and WILLIAM JOHNSON were indicted for a misdemeanor.
HENRY RICHARDSON . I am in partnership with Edward Jenkins—we are slop-sellers, and live in Penny-fields, Poplar. On the 6th of October, about half-past nine o'clock in the evening, I was about closing my shop—the prisoner James was passing the door—I was standing there—he said, "Well, tailor, how do you do?"—I asked him what ship he belonged to—he said he had come from Sheerness with the captain, and that he belonged to the ship Hartley —he came into my shop—I asked him if he wanted any clothes—he said yes, he did—I took him into a back shop—I asked what he had got a month—he said 3 l. 10 s., and the other man (meaning Johnson, who was at the door) had only 3 l. a month—I asked him if the other man wanted any clothes—he said no, he was too stingy—while we were talking Johnson came in—I was then serving James with clothes—I asked him what he had got to take—he said he was going to be paid next morning in Cheapside, and he appointed to meet me or my young man at the Sailors' Home on the following morning, to go and receive their wages, which the captain had appointed to pay them the following morning, at Laurie and Hamilton's—James got a pair of pilot trowgen, a waistcoat, and a pilot jacket—Johnson got a jacket and a cap—I let them have them on the supposition that they were going to be paid the next morning, the one 3 l. 10 s., and the other 3 l. per month—we are obliged to let sailors have clothes before they have their money—I expected to be paid the next morning, and I sent my young man to the Sailors' Home—they represented Laurie and Hamilton's to be the owners of the vessel, and that the captain was to meet them there to pay their wages—James said he had 12 l. or 13 l. to take.
Johnson. Q. Did I come into the shop? A. You came into the back wareroom—you joined with James, and said you had come up with the Hartley from Sheerness with the captain, and on that faith I let you hare the goods.
CHRISTOPHER CULL . I am the prosecutor's servant. I was ordered to go to the Sailors' Home, and on my way I met the prisoners—they had not got the clothes on which they had of my master—they spoke in their own language—I asked if they had been paid—they said, "No"—when they got to our shop they got half-a-crown each from me, which I got from the firm—they then said, if I would go with them to Laurie and Hamilton's they would get their money—I went with them by an omnibus—they said they wished me to go with them to see them get their money, and then they asked me for the half-crown each—I went with them to Laurie and Hamilton's—they told me to wait outside, and they would go in and get their money—they went in and came out, but did not bring me any money—I then went into the office by myself—I left the prisoners in Cheapside, and the next morning I went to the Sailors' Home, and could not see them—I went again in the evening, but they were not there—on my way back found the prisoners in the Back road, drinking and carousing—I had my suspicions, and spoke to them—they told me they had not been paid—I waited a good bit, and then they said the second mate had been to tell them they were to be paid at the Sailors' Home.
James. You asked me, on the Monday or Tuesday, if I wanted clothes, and I told you no, only a monkey jacket and trowsers; you made
us drink brandy and gin; I told you I was going to Chcapside to have my prize-money, and you wanted to know if I wanted 10 s.
HENRY RICHARDSON re-examined. When the prisoners were going away, with their clothes tied up, they asked me to give them something to drink, for they were cold, and I told my boy to get a shilling's worth of rum, and give them.
WILLIAM BEDWELL BRADFORD . I am captain of the Hartley. The two prisoners were seamen on board my ship—they had been discharged, and paid their wages, on the 21st of September—they had no wages to receive at Laurie and Hamilton's, or elsewhere.
The prisoner James put in a written defence, stating that he had told the prosecutor that he had received his wages, but had to receive some prizemoney at Somerset House, which was true, and after that they had been trusted with the clothes.
JAMES — GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined Two Months.
JOHNSON — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18421024-3038]
3038. MARY ANN WHITE was indicted for a misdemeanor.
THOMAS PADDEN . I keep a carpet and general drapery shop in Ratcliffe-highway. I know the prisoner—Mr. William Blair has been a customer of mine for eight years—he lives in Red Lion-street, Wapping—the prisoner came to my shop on the 10th of October, about three o'clock in the afternoon—she said, "Mr. Padden, I want seven yards of striped cotton shirting for my master, Mr. Blair"—she said, "My master told me you was to send a bill by me, and he will call in the evening and pay for it: he it gone into the City to get some money"—I let her have it, considering, from what she said, that she came from Mr. Blair—Mr. Blair had some shirts a day or two before—she returned in about half an hour, and said, "Mr. Padden, you have charged my master 1 d. a yard too much for the shirting; and have taken 1 d. a yard more than you charged him before; but he has sent me for seven yards more, and also for three yards of fine brown holland for my mistress's aprons;" and opening out the bill which I had at first given her, she said, "Master says you must put it all on this same bill; and he will call up and pay you this evening, or at least on Saturday morning"—I cut off the shirting and the holland, and she took it a way.
WILLIAM BLAIR . I live at Wapping. The prisoner had been in my service—I dealt with Mr. Padden some time before this occurred—I never sent the prisoner for seven yards of striped cotton, nor for three yards of Holland for my wife's aprons—I never sent her back to say Mr. Padden had charged 1 d. a yard too much for it.
FREDERICK JOHN WOOD . I am a pawnbroker. I produce this striped cotton shirting, which I took in pledge of the prisoner—I am certain she is the person.
MR. PADDEN re-examined. These are the same two parcels of striped cotton which I delivered to the prisoner.
Prisoner. I should not have done it, but I got into a woman's company who advised me to do it.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 16.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Four Months.
