NEW COURT.— Wednesday, November 2 nd, 1842.
Fifth Jury, before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18421024-3041]
3041. JAMES GOSBELL, EDMUND SMITH , and FREDERICK SHACKLEFORD were indicted for a misdemeanor.
MESSRS. CLAUKSON and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
DONALD CAMERON . I am in the service of Robert Milbourn, a silk warehouseman, in Newgate-street—he has no partner—on the 16th of May, Shackleford came, and asked whether we served country dealers—I said we did—he said he was commencing business at Maidstone, and wished to open an account at our house, he would pay part in cash, and give respectable references for the balance—he asked to be shown some silks—I showed him some—he selected a parcel, amounting to 81 l. 0 s. 8 d.—he produced this card, and was about writing the names of the referes cm the back of it, when I took him into the counting-house, to the counting-house clerk—when he got into the counting-house he wrote these names of the referees—( read)—Bachelard, George-street, Mansion-house; Ingram, Addle-street; and Earp, Fleet-street—on the other side of the card is "Waterloo-house, William Beaumont, Week-street, Maidstone"—Mr. Wells was in the counting-house—I said, "Mr. Beaumont, of Maidstone"—I left the card in the counting-house—Mr. Milbourn was not in at the time—Shackleford left instructions that the goods should be sent at six o'clock, to the Swanwith-two-Necks, Lad-lane—that was all that passed that day—the goods consisted often yards of black satin, sixteen of white satin, and other things—the porter took them that evening—Mr. Wells accompanied him, and received some money—on the next day, the 17th, Shackleford came again, Mr. Milbourn was there then, and sold the goods—he selected number of goods, which came to 77 l. 5 s. 7 d.—he left instructions that they were to be left at the Swan-with-two-Necks, Lad-lane, at six o'clock—he left an order for a box of artificial flowers—on the 23rd be came again, and bought some more goods, amounting to 14 l. 16 s. and the flowers were sent with them—I addressed him several times as Mr. Beanmont—Mr. Milbourn, in my hearing, addressed him as Mr. Beaumont—he came again on the 31st—he bought some more goods that day, consisting of handkerchiefs, shawls, scarfs, crapes, and satinet, in two parcels, which amounted to 71 l. 13 s. 3 d., and 14 l. 12 s. 6 d., making 86 l. 5 s. 9 d.—the 14 l. 12 s. 6 d. was to be paid in the middle of the next month—they were to be sent as before, to the Swan-with-two-Necks—there was a direction written on them, "R. W. Beaumont, draper, Maidstone"—on the 6th of July he came again—I sold him ninety-five yards of silk and other goods, amounting to 14 l. 12 s. 11 d.—they were to be paid for on the 1st of August—that parcel was to be sent to the George Inn, Borough, by his desire, not later
than four o'clock—it was directed to "R. W. Beaumont, draper, Maidstone"—throughout our dealings we always called him Beaumont, and he answered to that name—if I had believed he was not Beaumont I should not have let him have these goods—the total amount of the goods was 279 l., of which we have received 104 l., and a few shillings.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE. Q. Are you one of the persons who gave evidence in a case last Sessions, for obtaining these very goods under false pretences? A, lam—I gave the same testimony—Shakleford was the only person then indicted. ( See Eleventh Session, page 1105.)
JOHN WELLS . I am counting-house clerk at Mr. Milbourn's—Cameron introduced Shackleford to me by the name of Beaumont, and gave me this card—there were some references given to me—I remarked they were not trade references, and Shackleford remarked he was sorry he could not give trade references, as he was only now commencing business—among other parties referred to was one of the name of Bachelard—I went to him—I found him in an office at the back of the Mansion House, an office up one pair of stairs, with several clerks in it—it was called the "Cork Stuffing Company"—when I inquired of Bachelard, he said he had known him several years, he was a highly respectable young man, he knew he had 700 l. of his own, and he had lately married a young woman with considerable property, which could be made available if necessary—that he had houses in Sloane-street, and if he were us he would trust him to any amount—I went from there to Mr. Ingram—he told me he had had the same references that I had, and he had trusted him to the amount of 150 l., and he did not think we should do wrong in trusting him likewise—I then went to Mr. Earp, in Fleet-street—I got a good character of him there, though I did not see Mr. Earp, and on that I permitted the prisoner to have goods—on the evening of the 16th of May I went to the Swan-with-two-Necks with the first parcel of goods, which consisted principally of silks—I asked for Beaumont, and Shackleford appeared—he was in the coffee-room—he paid me 30 l. in cash, as agreed when he bought the goods—I went the next evening to the Swan-with-two-Necks again—I saw him again, and he paid me 25 l. on account of another parcel of goods left the same evening—I then saw him as Mr. Beaumont—on the 1st of June Shackleford called at the warehouse again, and he then paid me 35 l., which was for some goods sold the day before—on the 6th of July I received 14 l. 8 s. from Shackelford, and he wanted more goods, but Mr. Milbourn refused to let him have them unless he paid for them—there was a bill drawn on him for 122 l. 7 s., which has been dishonoured—the balance now due to us, is 174 l. 16 s. 7 d.—Freeborn took the goods at all times, and I went with him.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE. Q, You gave your evidence last time to the same import? A. Yes.
— FREEBORN. I was porter to Mr. Milbourn. On the 26th of May I took some goods to the Swan-with-two-Necks, in company with Mr. Wells—I do not know when was the next time I took any goods there, but I accompanied Mr. Wells, and took the goods he has described, to the Swan-with-two-Necks; and on one occasion I went to the George Inn, in the Borough, with some goods, directed for "Mr. Beaumont, draper, Maidstone"—I left them at the George.
FREDERICK WINSTONE . I was warehouseman to Mr. Minton, of Cheapside—he is a wholesale hosier—I remember on the 18th of May seeing
Shackleford at the warehouse, he said he was going to open a shop at Maidstone, and was desirous of opening an account with us—he gave us a card, with "R. W. Beaumont, draper, Maidstone," upon it, and on the back the names of two parties, whom he gave us for reference—Mr. Bachelard, George-street, Mansion-house, and Mr. Ingram, a warehouseman—he selected some goods, consisting chiefly of hosiery—after he had selected them he left the card, and asked if I would go for the reference, and said he would call again the next day—he said that Bachelard knew what money there was in the concern—that was all that passed on that first occasion—on the 19th he called again—he then selected hosiery, consisting of stockings and other things, and fancy goods—the whole amount was considerably above 100 l.—he asked if I had been to the reference—I said yes, I had—he took a portion of the goods with him on that day, and the rest were to be sent to Maidstone, by the carrier—we deal in flannel as well as hosiery, but there were no flannels in the parcel he selected that day.
Q. Before this had you been to the referees? A. Yes, on the 18th, after Shackleford called—I went first to Mr. Bachelard—I found him at a house in George-street—I asked him if he knew Mr. Beaumont—he said, yes, he had known him the last ten or twelve years, and more particularly during the last five years—he said Mr. Beaumont had lately married a lady of property, and Mr. Beaumont himself had house property at Chelsea, and 700 l, in cash, and he had also a friend who was ready and willing to back him with 1000 l, or more—he said he had not the least doubt that he was a strictly honourable and persevering young man, and he had no doubt he would do very well—this was in the Cork Stuffing Office—I told my master, and the goods were sent by Thomas Huddle, our porter, to the Swan-with-two-Necks—on the 20th of May Shackleford called again, and bought some more goods, and on that day he took a portion of the goods away with him in a cab, saying he was going over the water, to see the goods properly packed on Larkin's van to Maidstone, and he would save our porter the trouble, that he was going to Maidsfone to get a fresh list of goods that would be necessary to open the shop—the rest of the goods we sent—we saw saw him again afterwards, and he took more goods—I cannot tell what was the whole amount, but the books are here—( referring to them)—the amount of the first parcel, on the 19th of May, which went to the Swan-with-two-Necks, was 11 l. 1 s. 2 d.; on the 20th, 3 l. 8 s. 6 d.—that was the small parcel which he took with him—on the same day, the 20th, we sent goods to the George Inn, Aldermanbury; amounting to 24 l. 10 s. 1 d.—(there are two offices of Larkin's, one in the Borough, and one at the George Inn, Aldermanbury)—on the 21st be bought some more goods, and he said, "I will save you the trouble of sending the goods over the water, I will take them with me in a cab" —there were two pieces of bombazeen and other things bought on that occasion, also gloves, stockings, socks, and stocks—the whole amounted to 51 l. 13 s. 3 d.—he said he would bring a fresh list from Maidstone, and we were to see him again on the Monday, which would be the 23rd—on e 23rd he came and bought a small parcel of goods, amounting to 1 l. 4 s.—he took that away with him in his hand—he came again on the 24th, an bought goods to the amount of 16 l. 6 s. 9 d., which were sent to the George Inn, in the Borough—on the 28th he called again, and bought goods to the amount of 24 l. 11 s. 3 d.—he said he was going to the George Inn, in
the Borough, to see the goods properly packed, and he took them with him in a cab—that was the last parcel, and that was all he had of us—I had never known him by any other name than Beaumont, and all the parcels sent to those various places were addressed, "R. W. Beaumont, Maidstone."
GEORGE FREDERICK MINTON . I am a wholesale hosier and warehouseman, in Cheapside. On the 18th of May my young man brought a card into my counting-house, with "Beaumont, Maidstone," on it—Shackleford was not there at that time—I desired my young man to make inquiries, and on the following day, in consequence of those inquiries, I permitted the Mr. Beaumont, as I supposed, to have the goods—that was on the 19th, and on the Monday afterwards, which was the 23rd, I went to Mr. Bachelard's, at the Cork Stuffing Company's—Bachelard stated that he had known Mr. Beaumont ten or twelve years, that he was a very respectable young man; he knew he had 900 l. capital; he had seen that; and he had married a young lady who had a whole house in Sloane-street, and 300 l. a year, and he had a friend who would help him with one or two thousand pounds, or more, if he required it—the communication from Bachelard to me was satisfactory—I believed it to be true, otherwise I should not let Shackleford have the goods—I at last began to have some misgivings—in consequence of that, I desired Shackleford to be shown in to me, and he was so, a few days after the 23rd of May—he then gave himself the name of Ford, and represented himself as buying for Mr. Beaumont—I did not inquire into the state of the account from my young man till early in June—I was not aware it was so heavy till I received a letter from Beaumont, and a further order—I then called on Bachelard again—he offered to become surety for 50 l. if I would let him have more goods—I considered, and declined it—I went to Maidstone myself about the 21st or 22nd of June—I found the shop, with "Beaumont" over the door—I went into the shop, and saw two persons—one was Mr. Beaumont's father, as I was told, and the other was the prisoner Gosbell—I addressed myself to Gosbell, in consequence of his coming towards me—I asked him for Mr. Beaumont—he said he was in town, and would not return till Saturday—(this was Thursday)—I then asked for a Mr. Smith—Gosbell said he was gone to Chatham, and would return in the course of that afternoon—I waited in Maidstone the whole of that day, and went to the shop again after ten o'clock at night, when they were closing, in order to see Smith, but I could not, he had not returned—I then asked Gosbell if the buyer was down there—he said no, he never came to Maidstone—I asked him if he knew him—he said he did not, and did not know his name.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE. Q. Who did you see in the shop besides Gosbell? A. An elderly person—I thought I had seen him before, and I have since ascertained that it was Beaumont, sen.—I saw him afterwards at the Mansion-house, and recognised him as a person who had opened a shop some time before, and wanted to open an account with my house, which I had declined—I think I was at the Mansion-house on the first inquiry, after Shackleford was taken—I do not recollect whether I saw Gosbell the first time—I believe I attended every meeting at the Mansion-house—Gosbell certainly was there the second time, if not the first—he certainly was there on the second occasion—I cannot swear whether he was there on the first—he was no witness that I know of—I do not think he was examined at all—I do not know who he came there with—I saw
him standing, in the body of the Court—he was examined, certainly at one of the meetings—I really do not know whether there was any serive made on him to attend as a witness—I think it was agreed that Mr. Goddard was to write a letter to Mr. Gosbell to come up, but we got no information from him.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. DO you know of any written paper that was sent to Maidstone? A. No—I do not know the fact at all—it was merely a matter of discussion between myself and some other persons—we wanted to possess ourselves of all the information we could, and it was considered desirable to have Gosbell, not as a witness, but in consequence of one of the creditors fancying that Gosbell was only a servant there, he was invited to town, as he might give some information—I understood he was to be written to on that account—he came up, and returned to Maidstone, without our being able to elicit any thing from him—subsequently I saw him before the Magistrate—he was put on his defence, and I think he was required to give security for his appearance—he was bound in his own recognizance of 40 l.—I think that was on the second or third meeting—I think that he was bound to appear—there might have been several meetings in which he was not bound over, but I did not notice—I had no control over him—I do not think, after he was put on his defence, that he was discharged, except on his own recognizance—I left this to my attorney to manage—we were desirous of getting what information we could from those whom we believed to be innocently acquainted with the transaction.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE. Q. Did Shackleford refer you to any other person besides Bachelard? A. Yes, to Ingram—that reference was satisfactory as far as it went—he is a reputable tradesman.
JURY. Q. Did you receive any sum of money? A. Yes, 70 l —the balance due to me is 165 l. 5 s., in the name of Beaumont.
JOHN HUDDLE . I was porter to Mr. Minton—I delivered some goods on the 20th of May at the Swan-with-two-Necks, directed to Mr. Beanmont, of Maidstone—I took some to the George, Aldermanbury, on the 21st, and some to the George Inn, in the Borough, on the 23rd, and some at other times.
WILLIAM WEST . I am warehouseman to William Thomas and John Smart, silk manufacturers, in Milk-street, Cheapside. On the 2nd of June the prisoner Shackleford called there—I saw him—he handed a card to me with the name of "Robert William Beaumont, Maidstone," saying be wished to make a purchase—he gave references, and said be would call again in two hours—he gave the names of Bachelard, Ingram and Co., and Ridge, Milbourn, and Co, in Newgate-street—he came again, and as some of the parties were out I said he must call again—he said he was quite sure we should be satisfied with the references by making further inquiries, and he called again in the course of an hour—in consequence of the application to the references by Mr. Smart, we allowed him to open an account on that same day, the 2nd of June—I have the book here—he selected that day black silks, satins, and velvets to the amount of 83 l. 5 s. 5 d.—they were to be sent to the Swan-with-two-Necks, Lad-lane, by direction—we sent them addressed to "R. W. Beaumont, Week-street, Maidstone"—they were to be left at the bar of the Swan-with-two-Necks, Lad-lane, and they were done so—the porter took them—I saw Shackleford again on the 8th of June—he then had some silk velvets to the amount
of 1 l. 4 s.—he took them away himself—he came again about a week after the 8th, and looked out a parcel of goods, which I refused to let him have without having the money for them, or part payment for what he had formerly purchased—his answer was, that he would go home, and if they were wanted, he would remit money for them, or let us have part payment for what he had had—there the thing rested—he was given fully to understand that he would have no more goods without part payment—I never saw him again until he was at the Mansion-house—the amount of goods he had was 97 l. odd—I did not know him by any other name than Beaumont—I always addressed him by that name.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE. Q. Is it not common in trade to address a person who is a buyer for a mercantile gentleman, by the name of his employer, when you do not happen to know his name? A. Yes—but I addressed him in the name of the card he gave me—I presumed him to be Mr. Beaumont—the goods were destined for Maidstone, but were to be left at the Swan-with-two-Necks.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. YOU took, him for Beaumont, because he so represented himself? A. Yes—the goods were not to be taken to the coach office, but to be delivered at the bar of the Swan-with-two-Necks—whether they got to Maidstone I do not know—we have received no money.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE. Q. Have you attended to this prosecution? A. Yes—I have not attended any meeting of the creditors—I was present at the'first inquiry before the Magistrates—I think I saw Gosbell there then—I cannot say where he stood—he was passing in and out repeatedly—he was not in any particular part—I did not see him in communication with Mr. Goddard.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. When you were firef before the Magistrate did you know what share Gosbell had been supposed to have taken? A. No.
JENKIN JENKINS . I am porter to Messrs. Smarts'—I delivered the goods at the bar of the Swan-with-two-Necks, for Mr. Beaumont, of Maidstone.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE. Q. What goods were they? A. Silks and velvets—I carried one parcel—I read the direction on it, "R. W. Beaumont, Week-street, Maidstone"—I delivered it to the barman.
WILLIAM PATET . I am a perfumer, and live in Lombard-street. On the 25th of May, Shackleford came to my shop—he said be wanted some of our goods, if our prices and terms suited him—I said, "Very well, I dare say they will"—he gave me a card, "B. W. Beaumont, Waterloobouse, Maidstone," and on the back he wrote two references, Mr. Bachelard and a Mr. Yapp, I think, but I did not see him—I went to Mr. Batchelard's, but I saw it was only an office—I did not ring the bell—I returned, and said it was not a business reference, I wanted a business reference—he said, "I can satisfy you on that point," and he gave me two other names, one of which was Mr. Ingram, and said be would call in an hour—I went and saw Mr. Ingram, and the reference to him was such that I went home, and told my shopman to take Mr. Beaumont's order when he came—I supplied him with goods to the amount of 25 l. or 26 l.;—there was one item of 1 l., which he took with him the first day; and the others were sent to Week-street, Maidstone—he ordered a third parcel, but by some good luck we got it back again—the amount of our order, which is unpaid, is 26 s. or 27 l., I forget which—I have never got any thing for it—I certainly never knew him by any other name than Beaumont.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE. Q. YOU do not know him by any other
name now, do you? A. No—I gave him this credit on the reference to Mr. Ingram—I never knew Mr. Ingram, before—I did not like Mr. Bachelard's, because it was an office—our traveller takes a journey twice year, and it was understood that these goods were to be paid for when he called—I did not expect to be paid for them for six months—we executed another small order for him of 7 l., which we got back—I did not see him the second time he called.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE. Q. Did you go before the Magistrate? A. Yes—I have been acting from the first in conducting this prosecution—I was not aware of any meeting of creditors to advise on what should be done, or about having Gosbell up—I was before the Magistrate on the first inquiry—it was in July, I think—I recollect seeing Gosbell on that occasion—I did not speak to him, and I am not aware that he spoke to any person—I cannot tell whether he attended on the second examination by a summons—I know he attended and went away—I cannot remember when he gave his own recognizances, I think it was about the third time—he certainly did not attend as a prisoner.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. DO you know who he came with, or went away with? A. No—I think I heard he was required to be there; and on one occasion he was allowed to go on his own recognizances—I did not know what was to be attributed to Gosbell at that time—I trusted the whole of this to my attorney—from the representation made to me by Shackleford, from beginning to end, I believed him to be Mr. Beaumont, of Waterloo-house, Maidstone.
JAMES LANGLEY . I am porter to Mr. Patey. On the 1st of Jane I took a chest of goods, directed to Mr. Beaumont, Maidstone, to the King's Head, in the Borough—I left them there—I have got the signature for them.
CHARLES WILLIAM WAGSTAFF . I am salesman to Mr. John Mumford, of Milk-street, Cheapside, a lace warehouseman. On the 23rd of June, I remember Shackleford coming to my master's warehouse—he asked to look at some goods, which were shown him, and he purchased to the amount of 39 l. 9 s. 3 d.—they consisted of gauze handkerchiefs, blonde, bobbin-net, and other things—he gave his name "Mr. Beaument, Week-street, Maidstone"—he wrote it down himself, and I copied it into the day-book—he wrote down two references, Mr. Ingram, and Mr. Bachelard—of course we inquired of them—I went first to Mr. Ingram—I got there what was satisfactory as to his solvency—I then went to Mr. Bachelard, at the Cork Stuffing Company—Bachelard said that Beanmont was a very respectable man indeed, and possessed property to the amount of some thousands—we sent the first parcel I believe the same evening, after I had seen the referees—I believe they were sent to the George Inn, in the Borough—that was by his direction—be called again the same evening, between five and seven o'clock, after I had seen the referees—he asked if the parcel was gone—I believe it was not sent then, and he wished it to be sent by some particular hour that evening, as he was going—they were sent, directed to Mr. Beaumont, Week-street, Maidstone—I did not see Shackleford again till the 30th of June, when he called and purchased Lyons nets, fancy nets, Paris nets, and veils to the amount of 15 l. 8 s. 4 d.—he said he should let us have some money the next time he called—I think we sent that second parcel the day after, with the same direction, to the Borough—he called again, I believe, on the
11th or 12th of July, and looked out other goods to the amount of between 20 l. and 30 l.—while we were looking out these goods my employer, Mr. Mumford, came in, and I introduced Mr. Beaumont, as I supposed him to be, to him, as Mr. Beaumont, of Week-street, Maidstone, and I introduced Mr. Mumford to him, as Mr. Mumford, sen.—Mr. Mumford then asked Shackleford if he was Mr. Beaumont, asked him to give him his Christian name, said he would take it down, and asked whether he had any partner—he said he had not—he asked him if he was buying the goods on his own account—he said he was—he then looked out some goods to the amount of 20 l. or 30 l.—he was asked by me or Mr. Mumford, where they were to be sent to, but I do not recollect where he said they were to be sent—I do not recollect whether Mr. Mumford wrote down something about that, but I believe he did—I saw him write Beaumont's address—he asked him how the name Beaumont was spelt, and he asked him after he had written it on a paper if he had spelt it right—he said it was quite correct—I did not know Shackleford by any other name than the name he gave.
Cross-examined by Mr. PRICE Q. And you do not know him by any other name now? A. I suppose his name to be Shackleford—I do not think his name is Jenkins, because a plate was found on his person when he was taken, with the name of Shackleford on it, and also some cards—I was examined in Mr. Mumford's case last Sessions against Shackleford, and related the same story—I do not recollect which name was given in first, Ingram or Bachelard—I do not know how I came to go to Mr. Ingram first—I did not know anything of him, or of Mr. Bachelard—Shackleford obtained from us about 55 l. worth of goods altogether—the last parcel was not sent.
JOHN MUMFORD . I am warehouseman in Milk-street, Cheapside. On the 11th or 12th of July, I came into the warehouse, and saw Shackleford there, purchasing goods—Wagstaff was the salesman—he introduced Shackleford to me—he said, "Mr. Mumford, Mr. Beaumont"—tome goods had been arranged to be purchased—I said to Shackleford, "Your name is Beaumont?"—he said, "It is, my name is Beaumont"—I said, "Are you buying goods on your own account?"—he said he was—he then continued the purchase—when he was about to leave, I said, "Where will you have there goods sent to?"—he asked if they could be ready by six o'clock—I laid they could—he said, I wish them to be sent to me at the Railway Tavern"—I then asked him for his name, that it might be entered correctly in the books—I then wrote his name as he dictated—I showed it to him after I had written it, and he said it was right—he said if the goods were sent there at the time, he would pay 15 l.—this is the paper I wrote, and showed him—( read)—"Robert William Beaumont, Pool's Railway Tavern, at six o'clock; and send a receipt for 15 l."—I directed the goods to be sent—the porter went, and I went with him—when we got to Pool's Railway Tavern I did not find him—I ordered the goods to be taken home—I afterwards took him in a tobacconist's shop, in Fenchurch-street—he was about making a purchase of cigars.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE Q. Have you given us the same account as you did last Sessions? A. Yes—I believe he did not pay for the cigars—I have no means of knowing his name, but by what he gave—I have seen it written.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE Q. Were you present at any meeting of the creditors? A. I think I was—we had many conferences from the
26th of July to the 1st of August—Mr. Mintonand others were there—I do not remember whether it was at Mr. Goddard's office—Gosbell was there and was asked many questions—I cannot recollect any instructions given to Mr. Goddard to send to Maidstone—I accompanied Roe to Maidstone, and saw Gosbell there—he gave me some information—I do not recollect his offering to have his house searched—I remember Gosbell being examined, but I cannot tell when—I think Kimbrell was examined on the first inquiry—I do not know whether Gosbell was bound over before or after Kimbrell's evidence—I cannot recollect—there was a meeting of the creditors on the case of Shackleford at Mr. Goddard's office—Kirobrell was not present then—Gosbell was there, as a person assisting the prosecution—he from time to time gave information relative to it, and in consequence of that not proving satisfactory, he was prosecuted.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. You took Shackleford, and at first the proceedings were against him? A. Yes; and from time to time other information came out—Gosbell was examined at Mr. Goddard's office, and afterwards Kimbrell was sent for and examined.
GEORGE HERTSELL . I am porter to Mr. Mumford. On the 23rd of June I took a parcel to the George, in the Borough, addressed to Mr. Beaumont, of Maid-stone—I left it there.
HENRY WILLIAM INGRAM . I am a woollen warehouseman in Addle-street. On the 13th of May Shackleford came to my warehouse—he presented a card with a reference to Mr. Bachelard, in George-street, Mansion-house; "R. W. Beaumont, of Week-street, Maidstone," was on the card—he said he wished to purchase goods of me—he said he was about opening a shop at Maidstone—he said the shop was taken, and he wanted some goods—I did not see him write the name of the referee—I promised to go to Bachelard—I went there that day—I saw him, and he said he knew Beaumont, that he had about 600 l. or 800 l. in the business, and his wife had some property in houses in Sloane-street—I was satisfied of the solvency of Beaumont, from the account be gave me—Shackleford looked out goods that day, part woollen goods, and part trowsers—they amounted to 50 l. 5 s.—they were sent to the Swan-with-two-Necks, by his direction—my porter, James Steel, took them—I will not say how they were addressed—on the day after, there was another parcel to the amount of 56 l. 14 s. 6 d.—he paid my salesman 20 l. on the 13th, and in all he paid 80 l.—106 l. 3 s. 6 d. is the balance remaining due—the witness came to me from Mumford's and Patey's for a reference—I was induced to give a satisfactory account of Beaumont, by having a reference myself, and being paid part of my account.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE. Q. You had no intention of assisting the person who applied to you, to obtain these goods? A. Not in the least—I gave the character which I thought he had, honestly and fairly—others may have done the same thing—I gave my opinion, and thought they might do as they liked—I was examined last sessions on Shackleford's trial.
MR. DOANE Q. Did you believe all through that Shackleford, whom you believed was Beaumont, was a respectable man? A. Yes.
GEORGE MILLS . I am in Mr. Ingram's employ—I went down to Maidstone in May, about three weeks after the account was opened—I found a shop there, partly fitted up—I saw Gosbell there—I told him I came from Mr. Ingram's—I asked to see Mr. Beaumont—Gosbell said Mr. Beaumont had left that morning for town, and most likely he would call on Mr. Ingram—I asked
him his opinion of Mr. Beaumont's respectability—he laid he believed Mr. Beaumont to be a very respectable man, having married a lady of considerable property, that, if he had any property, and Mr. Beaumont wished for a partner, he should have no objection to place his money at Mr. Beaumont's disposal for business, he would be very happy to join him in business—I thought there was a deficiency in the shop, and I stated there must have been a great quantity of goods bought in London to my knowledge, and I wanted to know where they were—there was not sufficient in the shop to satisfy me—he said they did not receive the goods in the shop entrance, but at the side door, and immediately passed them into the cellar—I did not go into the cellar.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE. Q. You asked Gosbell what he was? A. Yes; he said he was a man employed by Mr. Beaumont—I asked if he was the managing man—he said there was a Mr. Smith there, bat he was out, or had left for London, or something to that effect—Mr. Beaumont's name was over the shop-door—I had heard, before I went down, that there was such a shop at Maidstone, and I wrote there to ascertain the fact—when I returned I went to Bachelard's—what Gosbell had stated was carried out by what Bachelard stated, and I sent more goods—I had a conversation with one creditor, who said that the parties were respectable.
MR. DOANE. Q. Who was that creditor? A. Mr. Minton—I sent more goods to Beaumont, after what Gosbell had told me about his marrying this lady.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE. Q. Did you not know that Beaumont was only commencing business? A. Yes; but I knew what he represented, and how many goods he had bought, and I knew that goods ought to be in the shop if he intended to open business—I first went to Maidstone by Mr. In gram's direction; and again, in a fortnight, of my own accord—Gosbell appeared to be shopman—I do not know that Beaumont has married a person of property—I did not know Bachelard till I called on him—I called on him several times, and I met him in the street—Gosbell answered me candidly and fairly, as far as I could judge, some questions, and some he did not—be told me that the person in the shop, who I knew to be old Mr. Beaumont, was another name—I only knew about Mr. Beaumont from what I heard.
JAMES STEEL . I am porter to Mr. Ingram. I took some goods for Mr. Beaumont, of Maidstone, to the Swan-with-two-Necks, on the 13th of May—I left them there.
WILLIAM KIMBRELL . I was pot-boy, at the Bull and Mouth tap in July—I lived there six months—I left in the summer, to go to the Geeat Western Railway—I have been there about three months—while I was at the Bull and Mouth, Shackleford used to come there—he used to go by the name of Mr. Shackleford—Smith and Gosbell used to come there, and also a person who went by the name of Beaumont—they used to come very often—they frequented the parlour together—sometimes they came one or two together, and sometimes they came separately, and then inquired for the others—we had brown paper parcels brought to the house by porters—I saw one of the porters here just now—some parcels I have had to take care of for the prisoners—Smith and Beaumont have given them me to take down stairs—Gosbell and Shackleford have been there at the time, and five or six persons in the room—I have seen Smith open some parcels—they contained silk handkerchiefs, and scarfs with
knots tied in the fringe, crape shawls, coarse shawls, and silk velvets—I have seen them move them about on the tables, and then tie them up again—I have seen Smith pull out a coarse bag with a great deal of money in it, and put it on the table, and they were receiving some—what he gave them I cannot say—I have seen Shackleford there at the time, and another short gentleman, when I have seen Smith counting out the money to them, while they stood round a table, and a pen and ink and some paper I brought in—the goods were then taken away—I took one parcel for Smith, and put it in the buss, and he gave me sixpence—sometimes the goods have been put into cabs —Smith, Shackleford, Gosbell, and a short gentleman, have been present when the goods have been taken out of the room—I do not know any public-house in Barbican, but Smith has told me if any one called to see Mr. Beaumont to say he was gone to Barbican—I have had bits of paper from him to tell that, and if any one came, and inquired for Mr. Smith, or Mr. Shackleford, I have given them the pieces of paper—they were in the habit of coming to the honse very nearly all the time I lived there—my mistress was not satisfied with one or two things, and she forbid them all the house—I heard her forbid Shackleford the house, and would not serve him.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE Q. Perhaps he could not pay hit bills? A. He always paid very well—there was no waiter but me—there was a female servant—she has served Mr. Shackleford with half-a-pint of wine, when he has been up stairs with his lady, and I perhaps was out of the way at the time, cleaning the pots—I do not know her name—she did not wait on company, only when I was busy—I have had nothing given me for coming here—I should not have come if I had not been made to come—I am now engaged at the Great Western Railway, looking after some horses for Mr. Saunderson—I lived with my father at Buckingham five years ago, and helped him in the stables—I then went to live with an aunt at Baltown—I have lived with Mr. Chantry, at Colchester—I misbehaved myself, and he dismissed me—I then went to Mr. Saunderson, who sent me to the Bull and Mouth tap—I was there six months—I cannot say when these gentlemen first began coming backwards and forwards—many strangers used to come—I cannot tell the first or last day I saw either of the prisoners—they came frequently before I, left—I cannot say how often—I took no more notice of them than of other customers—I noticed the parcels—other persons took notice of these things as well as I did—a mail guard did, and Mr. Smith pulled off his coat to fight him—the guard came and told my mistress that she ought not to encourage them there, they were a pack of swindlers, and nothing else—Mrs. Saunderson was my mistress—she is not here—she did not attend on the gentlemen in the parlour, that was my business—it was at the bar that she forbade Mr. Shackleford the house—they never went into any room but the coffee-room—Mrs. Saunderson might take a thing in now and then—perhaps I have been in seven or eight times, in the course of the day, answering the bell—Mr. Shackleford never used to go and sit in the bar—mistress did not like him well enough—she did not approve of him, nor of any of them, so she said—I never saw any customers sit down in the bar—there is a bar maid—she may have gone into the coffee-room if I was out of the way—I heard my mistress tell Mr. Shackleford that she did not wish him to come there "at all, and she has said to me that she wished they would go somewhere else, and have their things—it was
Mr. Smith that showed me the handkerchiefs—he opened them—I do not know whether it was for the express purpose of showing them to me—there were five or six parcels opened at the same time on the table—he did not say why he showed them to me—he said they were beauties—he opened them, and pulled them one off the other—I took something into the room at the time—I was against the table, looking at them—I was not called to look at them—they were tied up again, and taken away.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE Q. When did you first hear that you would be wanted as a witness against these parties? A. I heard nothing about it till a man came after roe at the Great Western Railway—I do not at all recollect when it was, but he said I must come to the Mansion-house, at eleven o'clock next morning, to give evidence—it was more than a month after I left the Bull and Mouth—I should not think it was two months—the man brought me a paper—when the Lord Mayor asked me if I knew the prisoner, I said yes—no one had spoken to me before that—I cannot tell how long after I had been at the Bull and Mouth I first saw either of the three prisoners, or which I first saw—I did not know there were two Mr. Smiths—there was Mr. Beaumont, a stout gentleman—I cannot say how many times I have seen Mr. Smith bring paper parcels there, but very frequently—I have seen him bring them in and take them away under his arm—I cannot say when I first saw him bring any parcels—I saw two others, besides Mr. Smith, bring parcels—one was a man with a wen on his cheek—I do not recollect a man named Ketcher—I cannot tell when it was that I carried the parcel for Mr. Smith, for which he gave me 6 d.—I cannot say how long it was before I left—I do not know that I should know the handkerchiefs again which Mr. Smith showed me—some were bright yellow ones—( looking at some produced by Mr. Ballantine)—I could not swear to these—I will not say either way——( looking at some others)—I cannot swear to these—they may be brought out of the shop like them, and then I should not know—( looking at others)—these are like the handkerchiefs I saw, but they may not be them—Mr. Smith opened the yellow ones to show me—I took particular notice of them—I do not know a person named Bates, who was in the habit of coming to the house—I cannot say whether any parcels were brought on the 9th of July, or on the 11th, 12th, or 13th—I took no notice of the time, nor can I say when these handkerchiefs were shown to me—my mistress might have been very good friends with Mr. Smith—I saw none of it—he was a very good customer, for aught I know, he always paid for what he had—she did not like any of them, and did not study their custom—I cannot recollect that she mentioned any names when she said she did not like any of them.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE Q. I suppose you went out on errands from time to time? A. If mistress wanted me to go—I was discharged from my service at Colchester for stopping out till eleven o'clock, or half-past, when I ought to have been home at ten; and also I met with two or three accidents, I broke a pony's knees—there are plenty of ostlers in the yard, at the Bull and Mouth, employed to look after the coaches and busses —the prisoners used to go into the coffee-room—Mr. Shackelford had a private room, and used to go up stairs with a young woman—Gosbell used to go into the coffee-room—that is a public room—I cannot tell when it was I have seen him there—I do not know what month it was in—he used to be there frequently along with Shackelford, Smith, and
Beaumont—he came along with the rest, at the same time as they came—I cannot say whether it was in January, February, March, or when it was—I never kept any account of when he was there, when he came, or when he went away—I have seen him there along with the rest—I have seen other people come with these parties from time to time—other persons used the house—I cannot say that I have not had luggage to take care of for a gentleman, but I had plenty of parcels for these gentlemen.
MR. DOANE. Q. Was it just before you left the Bull and Mouth that you saw these people, and the others you have named, frequenting the house? A. It was not long before—they used to come there three or four times a day sometimes—I saw them till I did leave—Mr. Sherman is the owner of the Bull and Mouth—Mr. Saunderson is his son-in-law—I am in the same service now.
WILLIAM SWANSON . I am porter at the Swan-with-two-Necks. I have seen Shackleford there—I first saw him there towards the end of May last—he asked if I had any parcels for Mr. Beaumont, of Maidstone—I said I had one, it was a box—I gave him the parcel, directed to Mr. Beaumont, of Maidstone—he left it in my care again, and went into the coffee-room—he came again the same day, and took it away in a cab —a great many more parcels came directed to Mr. Beaumont, of Maidstone—I saw Shackleford daily for a fortnight or three weeks after—the parcels were sometimes boxes, and sometimes brown paper parcels, sometimes small trusses—they were always taken away by Shackleford, with the exception of three, which were carried away by a porter—Shackleford was there when the porter came for the first, and of course he had the others by Shackleford's directions—one or two letters came directed to Mr. Beaumont, which were left at the bar—they were given to Shackleford—I saw him open one or two—be generally took parcels away in the cab —I addressed him as Mr. Beaumont, and he always answered to that name—there is a gateway going into Lad-lane—I always fetched the cabs for Shackleford, with the exception of one or two—the cab was driven up the gateway into the yard to the door of the hotel, the goods were put in, and Shackleford drove off—people passing could not see what was going on up the gateway, unless they stopped to look up the yard—I saw him there for a fortnight or three weeks, sometimes once a day, sometimes twice a day—he always came to fetch parcels for Beaumont, of Maidstone. Cross-examined by MR. PRICE Q. You saw no others there taking any part in this? A. No; there were others—I do not know that Shackleford's name is not Beaumont—when a man has luggage to take away, the cabs generally stop in the street—if we fetch a cab for a gentleman, we leave it outside—all this was done under Shackleford's direction.
MR. DOANE Q. There were others with him? A. I saw one or two in company with him, but that was generally in the coffee-room—I never spoke to them—I have seen him in company with the same persons more than once—the parcels were not sent to Maidstone, because Shackleford called and desired all parcels directed to Beaumont, of Maidstone, to be taken care of.
THOMAS HARRIS . I am a grocer, and live at Maidstone—I know Shackleford. In the beginning of last June I was engaged as porter by a man named Beaumont, who is not in custody, to attend to a draper's shop in Week-street Maidstone—it was opened the day I went, which I believe was on Saturday,
the 11th of June—there were two Beaumonts, the father and son—I saw them both, and Mr. Gosbell and a Mr. Smith, but not this prisoner—they all appeared to act as shopmen and masters—it was some time before I knew who was master—Gosbell was assisting in the shop as shopman—the stock of the shop appeared to consist but of few goods—I remained there till the shop was closed, which was some time about the middle of August—I only know of two parcels coming down during that time—I did not see any silks or velvets in the shop—there were four shawls hanging up, I did not see any more—customers came and asked for articles they could not be supplied with, and went away without being served—Beaumont and Smith were there till the Wednesday in the first week—they then left, and came back again on Saturday evening—they went away again, I suppose on the Monday, and returned on the following Saturday, about six o'clock in the evening, with two ladies—Shackleford came with them—I shut up the shop that night between eleven and twelve o'clock, and I went home—I asked Gosbell what time I should come on Monday morning to open shop—he went into the room behind the shop, where Smith, the two Beaumonts, and Shackleford were, to inquire—he came back, and said I was not wanted till eight o'clock—I went on the Monday morning about eight—the shop was open—Gosbell was there, and the elder Beaumont—I did not see any others—Beaumont told me to dust the window, and then he said I was not wanted, I might look in about noon—I left the shop—I returned between twelve and one o'clock, and found the elder Beaumont there—on the following Saturday young Beaumont and Smith came down in the last buss, about half-past nine in the evening—they staid about half-an-hour, and then wept away—I have not seen them since—Gosbell and old Beaumont continued at the shop—old Beaumont remained there till the shop was closed, which was in the middle of August, on a Thursday—I closed the shop on the Wednesday night—Beaumont desired me to get a cart to remove the goods, and said he should not open the shop any more—I got a cart the following morning to remove the remainder of the stock, and what furniture there was, to a house which his wife had taken opposite the barracks—I went there with a cart and donkey at five o'clock—I found old Beaumont there—he directed me, and I took the things to this house opposite the barracks—there was some hosiery and other linendrapery goods, there were six boxes in all—there was very little furniture, I suppose altogether it was not worth 50 l.—after Smith and Beaumont had left the last time, I saw a decrease in the quantity of stock, from the shelves—some pieces that hung about the shop for making dresses were gone—young Beaumont's wife was there for three or four days—no goods were unpacked in the cellar while I was there—I went into the cellar several times, I never saw any goods there—I had to go there every evening, to turn the gas on and off.
Cross-examined by MR. PRICE Q. Have there not been some injurious reports about the Beaumonts? A. There had been; two gentleman came down one morning, and were talking there for three quarters of an hour—Beaumont left Maidstone before I heard any rumours—the goods were removed openly in an uncovered cart, and we were there till ten o'clock.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE Q. What was your occupation when you first went there? A. The first thing I did was to clean the windows—I attended there every day, and went round with some bills all round the town—I had to go out with goods when any went out—they used to tell
me every evening whether they wanted me, then I did what was wanted, and looked in again—I knew nothing of them previous to the 11th of June—there were four or five mats in the cellar which had had furniture packed in them—I did not see matting round the furniture—I was the only servant in the house—I was cook—there was a woman there on the first Saturday—I set up grocer again as soon as I could get a situation—I am not a grocer on my own account—Gosbell did not continue in the house so long as Mr. Beaumont—Goshell did not leave directly after Mr. Mumford came down—I do not recollect his leaving till one Friday afternoon, which must have been the latter end of July.
MR. DOANE Q. What were the rumours about this shop? A. I heard they had been obtaining goods under false pretences, which I believe was current in the newspapers—there was no regular female servant—the whole of the house was not furnished, only the sitting-room at the back of the shop, and two rooms up stairs—each had a bed in them—there was no servant beside me—I was there day by day, from the 11th of June till the Wednesday in August when the shop was closed—I was paid in the evening for the day.
Cross-examined by MR. WILDE Q. Do you remember when Gosbell came? A. No, I found him there on the Saturday I went there—he was there but very little the last three weeks—I think he was there the night before the shop closed.
WILLIAM MORRIS ( City police-constable.) I found Shackleford in custody of Mr. Mumford, in a tobacconist's in Fenchurch-street—I took him to the station, and found on him a plate and some cards engraved, which are "F. Shackleford, wine and spirit-merchant, Brighton, Dealer in cigars of the first quality"—I have looked after Charles Sidney Smith, Bachelard, and the two Beaumonts, without success.
JAMES BRANNAN ( police-sergeant G 20.) I have had a warrant against Charles Sidney Smith, Bachelard, and the two Beaumonts, and have looked diligently for them, in company with inspector Shackle night after night.
JOSEPH SHACKLE . I am a police-inspector. I have searched with Brannan, and in vain.
JURY to WILLIAM KIMBRELL. Q. You saw some parcel opened; did you see whether it was directed at all? A. No, I saw some were directed, but I did not see this one.
(William Green, a linen draper, of Church-street, Hackney, gave Gosbell a good character.)
GOSBELL — GUILTY . Aged 29.
SMITH — GUILTY. Aged 45. Judgment Respited.
SHACKLEFORD — GUILTY . Aged 36.
ESSEX CASES.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18421024-3042]
3042. EDWARD BAKER was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of James Roberts, on the 14th of October, at West Ham, and stealing therein, 1 basket, value 1 s.; 25 spoons, value 7 l.; 16 forks, value 8 l.; and one fish-slice, value 15 s., his goods; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
CAROLINE REYNOLDS . I am servant to Mr. James Roberts, of Uptonplace,
West Ham. On the afternoon of the 14th of October, on going into the parlour, I noticed the shadow of a figure on the side-board—I was alone in the house—I looked through the door, and saw the prisoner standing against the dining-room table, in the middle of the room, with the plate-basket in his hand—on seeing me, he turned round, looked at me, and jumped out of the window, taking the plate-basket with him—I followed, and seized hold of him as he was getting over the gate leading into the back garden—I got possession of the plate-basket—I do not recollect whether he relinquished his hold, or whether I took it from his hand, but all in a moment I found the basket in my possession—I laid hold of his coat after I got the basket, and called out "Murder" and "Police"—I felt him, as I thought, shifting to get his coat off—I then let go my hold of the skirts of his coat, took up the plate-basket, went into the back garden, and gave an alarm—he was brought back in about three-quarters of an hour or an hour—I am sure he is the same man—he left his hat behind him, and in it were several religious and other tracts, and a sheet almanack—a coat was also brought to me, which was such a one as he had when I caught hold of him—the plate-basket had been on the side-board before I saw the prisoner with it—the window was at that time shut down—I found it open when I saw him standing in the parlour—the door of the house was shut—there was no other way of getting in except through the window—I do not know how he would get to the parlour window without getting over the wall and the iron palisades—the plate-basket contained the articles stated, which are worth upwards of 20 l.—it is my master's property—I gave information to a man named Miller, who went in pursuit—he is too unwell to be here.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE Q. You were very much alarmed when you saw the prisoner? A. Yes—I had an opportunity of seeing his face while I was struggling with him, and I saw him through the crack of the door before I went into the parlour—I knew him again directly I saw him.
JOHN WILCOX ( police-constable K 368.) On the afternoon of the 14th of October, I was crossing the fields towards Upton, and saw the prisoner jump over the fence of Mr. Gurney's park, which is, I should say, about three-quarters of a mile from the prosecutors premises—a great number of people were pursuing him—a conductor of the Parcel's Delivery Company stopped him in the park—I took hold of him, and asked why he ran—he said, because other people ran—he was the first man—a person named Miller came up when I got him to the station—in consequence of what he said, I took him to the prosecutor's house, and Reynolds said he was the man that took the plate-basket out of the parlour—he said, "Women must say something; I suppose I must hear it"—this hat was found in the garden, containing tracts and other things, such as people hawk about, and the coat was found in the next house—the prisoner denied that either were his—he put them on at Lambeth-street—he had no hat or coat on when I stopped him.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you help him on with them at Lambeth police-ofiice? A. No—one of the officers did.
MARIA THING . I am the wife of Mr. Robert's groom. I heard somebody call out, and ran up the garden—I saw the prisoner come quickly out of the water-closet—he nearly pushed me down—his hat came off, and he shifted one arm off his coat—he went over the wall, and got away into the next garden.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
ROBERT SUTTLF .( police-constable B 97.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—I was present at his trial, and know him to he the person described in this certificate—( read.)
GUILTY .* Aged 29.— Transported for Fifteen Years.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18421024-3043]
3043. ALICE WALKER and ELIZA SMITH were indicted for stealing, on the 14th of October, 70lbs. weight of coals, value 1 s., the goods of William Fredcrick Brown.
WILLIAM FREDERICK BROWN . I had two tons of coals in a gravel hole at the Eastern Counties Railway—I am the contractor—these coals produced resemble mine in every respect.
JOHN PHILPOT . At half-past six o'clock in the morning of the 14th of October I was in my field at Barking—I saw the two prisoners coming from Mr. Brown's place, carrying something heavy across the fields—I followed—they each had a bag—I went to Smith's house, and asked what they had in their bngs—they said only a few cinders which they had picked up on the railway—I found they had about 70lbs. Of coals, which exactly resembled Mr. Brown's.
Walker's Defence. I saw these bits of coals lying on a piece of waste ground, and next morning we went and picked them up.
Smith's Defence. These were pieces that had dropped about; we took them from where there was no person's property.
WALKER — GUILTY . Aged 52.
SMITH — GUILTY . Aged 27.
Confined Two Months.
KENT CASES.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18421024-3044]
3044. HARRIET PRUE was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of September, at St. Nicholas, Deptford, 1 pair of sugar tongs, value 12 s.; I rings, value 1 l.; 2 sheets, value 1 l.; 1 coat, value 3 l.; 1 pair of trowsers, value 1 l.; 1 waistcoat, value 10 s.; 3 gowns, value 1 l. 14 s.; 1 cloak, value 1 l. 10 s.; 3 handkerchiefs, value 6 s.; 1 veil, value 4 s.; and 2 shawls, value 1 l. 15 s.; the goods of Charles Skeet, in his dwelling-house.
ELIZABETH SKEET . I am the wife of Charles Skeet, and live at No. 7, Broomfield-row, St. Nicholas, Deptford—my husband rents the whole house. The prisoner came to lodge at our house at the latter end of July, as near as I can say—she said she was the wife of Hodges, a policeman, who came to live there with her—I thought they were man and wife—mj husband is a ship-keeper, and in the habit of going from home—I used to go to him on Sunday to spend the day, and the prisoner and Hodges were left in the house—I had noticed the prisoner many times leave the house with a basket and bundle when Hodges was at home—I have asked her at different times what she was taking away—she said she was going to the tailor's to get the police clothes altered—I asked her if she was going again—she said, "Yes," for they were very uncomfortable—the last time she left the house she borrowed a basket of me, and said she should be back on Tuesday to tea, but she did not return—I did not see her
again till she was in custody—I went to my drawers, and missed the sugar tongs, also some rings, my husband's clothes, my mother's large cloak, a silk shawl and veil, a pair of sheets, two pillow-cases, two gowns of ray mother's, a silk dress of my own, and other articles, worth together 10 l. or 15 l.—they may have been taken in less quantities than 5 l. value at a time—my husband's clothes were safe on the Sunday before she left, the cloak was in the same chest; and on the Tuesday, when I went to them, I found they were gone—the prisoner was then gone—when she came to my house she had nothing but a very small parcel in her hand—she said her trunk was coming, but it never came.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. Was Hodges living with her up to the last? A. Yes, up to the very last day of her going away—she left on Tuesday the 13th, and he came back on the Wednesday morning—ours is a four-roomed house.
MARY ASWELL . I have seen the prisoner leave the house with bundles from time to time—on the 12th of September I saw her go out with a large and small bundle, and after that I heard her say to a woman opposite where I live, that she was going away the next day.
JAMES WAINSCOT . I am a policeman of Hereford. On Sunday evening, the 18th of September, I apprehended the prisoner at her mother's house at Hereford—I told her I was going to take her to the station, on a charge of felony, for robbing her lodging—she said she knew nothing of it—I asked her where her box was—she said it had been by Cook's ran, and was left on the road—on the Tuesday morning I received the box out of the van, and took it to the station—the superintendent cautioned her not to answer, and asked her if the box was hers—she said it was—I found this silk dress and crape shawl in it—I asked if it was hers—she said yes, she had bought it for 10 s., of one John Elgar, who was in the habit of coming to Skeet's house, and who said it belonged to his wife, who was dead.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you found the box at Cirencester? A., Yes, in consequence of what she said—it was opened in her pretence.
HENRY DIMOND . I am a policeman. I was sent into the country to apprehend the prisoner—she told me the silk dress she bought of Elgar and she was to give 10 s. for it—that the had given him 3 s. and was to give him the rest afterwards.
JOHN ELGAR . I am a seaman, and live in Tooley-street—I never sold the prisoner anything—I take my meals at Skeet's—I was only there once since the things were lost—I had my meals down stairs, in the front room—Mrs. Skeet was there at the time.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know the prisoner? A. Only from seeing her there—I had my breakfast, dinner, and tea there—I never slept there—I never sold the prisoner anything whatever—I am positive I did not sell her this silk dress.
COURT Q. Do you know Hodges, who passed as her husband? A. Yes—I have seen him there—he was dressed as a policeman—I never sold her a gown—I never sold her any thing—she never gave me any money, and I never gave her any clothes.
MR. DOANE Q. Have you not, on one or more occasions, called at Skeet's on a Sunday? A. Not since the things were stolen—I cannot say, exactly, what time it was—I have been to the door, but not in the house—I did not owe a public-house score on the Sunday night I called—I swear that I did not say so—I had been in the habit of calling or going by the
house for several Sundays before, but not of going in—I never went into the house on a Sunday night after I left Mrs. Skeet's—I cannot tell when I ceased to board there—I only went to the door to pass the compliment of the day with the prisoner—I was acquainted with a great many more in that row—I swear that I never sold any thing to the prisoner on a Sunday I did not sell her the gown and scarf, and tell her not to say anything to "mother" about it as I owed a score at a public-house—I used to call Mrs. Skeet mother—I did not sell the things to the prisoner for 10 s. and receive 3 s.
COURT Q. Did you go there on a Sunday, before you ceased to board at Skeet's? A. Yes—I used to call at the door in general—Mrs. Skeet was not on shore till the evening—I used to come ashore with her and walk up to the house with her.
ELIZABETH SKEET re-examined. Elgar took his meals at my house—the property was in my bed-room—he was not there, to my knowledge, after the Sunday before the prisoner left, but I was with my husband on the Sunday on board a ship—I never noticed any intimacy between the prisoner and Elgar—Hodges is here—no other seaman but Elgar was in the house—I have traced no more of the property—I do not know what has become of the basket I lent her—she had her own basket, my basket, and a bundle, when she went out on Tuesday—she said she should return to tea, and asked me to keep the tea-kettle by the fire until seven o'clock, when she should come—Hodges was there on Monday and Tuesday—the prisoner always paid for the lodging.
MR. DOANE Q. Had not she given you reason to believe that she was going to return to her mother in the country? A. Yes—as she said Hodges was going into business again—she said she was going on the Wednesday, but she went on Tuesday—she said she expected a post-office order from her mother—she said if the order was there, waiting for her, she should return that night, about seven o'clock, and if not, I was to wait till next morning—Hodges said he was to meet her at Westminster-bridge at ten, if she did not return at seven—I heard nothing about her having to go to Mr. Smith's, in Whitecross-street, or that her fare was to be paid at the office at Hereford—she said she was going to stop there four or five days—Elgar owes me 2 s. 7 d. for trifling things—he told me that he had been to my house on a Sunday, but I did not see him—he bad his victuals with us on Sundays on board a ship—he sometimes went on shore, and I have heard that he has called at our house—the prisoner said so—I asked how long he stopped—she said "Oh, he did not stay above a quarter of an hour, he was rather in liquor."
COURT. Q. Where did you lose your two sheets from? A. From the linen-chest in the room I occupied—all the things I lost were from my room—Hodges was a policeman of the R division, and acted as such until the last day, (Tuesday)—he was taken up on this charge, and let out on bail, I believe—he gave up his police situation on that Tuesday, and gave up his clothes the very day she left—Hodges was at home and in bed at times, when the prisoner carried out bundles, as he was on night duty—my mother's things were in the same box—I had told the prisoner I did not go to that box, as I could not bear to look at the things, having recently lost my mother—Elgar did not know where I kept them—he was never up stairs to my knowledge.
JAMES WAINSCOT re-examined. I have known the prisoner five or six years, and knew her mother—she always bore a good character at Hereford—she left there six or eight months ago, I do not know why—I
have known Hodges all his life, he is a Hereford man—he was not a policeman at Hereford—he assisted the prisoner's husband in business, who is a butcher—Hodges left Hereford about the same time as the prisoner—his wife goes out washing—Cirencester is forty or fifty miles from Hereford—I searched the prisoner's mother's house before I got the box—she told me she had bought them of Elgar, and told me the name of the ship he might be found at.
HENRY DIMOND re-examined. Hodges resigned from the R division of police on account of getting something better to do—he gave up of his own accord—he had been about five months in the force.
GUILTY of stealing under 5 l. Aged 21.— Recommended to mercy by the prosecutrix. — Transported for Seven Years.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

JOHN BARR . I am shopman to Mr. Moore, a pawnbroker, at Woolwich—this rug was pawned by a female—I do not know who—this is the duplicate I gave to the person.

Prisoner's Defence. I pledged the rug, but intended to redeem it.

3050. JOHN WALLACE was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of October, 1 pewter pot, value 1 s., the goods of Henry Edwin Jaegers.
ALEXANDER M'NOUGHER . I am in the service of Henry Edwin Jaggers, who keeps the Artillery Canteen at Woolwich. On the 22nd of October I heard a noise, and went into the tap-room, and saw the prisoner with a pint pot beside him—there was no one else there—when he was gone the pot was gone—I told my mistress—I went to the prisoner's house and asked for the pint pot—he shoved me to the door, and shut it—I said "I must have it"—he went back, gave it me up, and said, "For God's sake don't say anything about it, or you will ruin me."
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE Q. How long was he there? A. Not two minutes—I knew the prisoner's name—I believe he works for my father—I do not know his son intimately—I have seen him often—I do not know that he and his father quarrelled—the prisoner lived about a quarter of a mile from my master's house—he has lived there four years—he is a brush-maker—I went to the prisoner's four minutes after he left—no one was present when he gave up the pot—I swear he did not say he was sorry he had kept the pot so long, he hoped there would be no piece of work about it—he had not had beer in it.
EDWARD PALMER ( police-constable R 231.) I took the prisoner, and told him I wanted him—he said, "What for? the pint pot?"—I said, "Yes"—he said, "For God's sake, be as lenient as possible; I never did such a thing before, and how I came to do it now I don't know."
Cross-examined. Q. Are these your depositions? A. Yes—I mentioned about being as lenient as possible before the Magistrate—I heard him say afterwards he could not believe he could do such a thing.
NOT GUILTY .

3051. THOMAS BEAUMONT was indicted for stealing, on the 13th of October, 1 purse, value 2 d.; 2 half-crowns, 2 shillings, 2 sixpences, 2 pence; 1 five-franc piece, value 4 s. 2 d.; and 2 francs, value 1 s. 8 d.; the property of Angus M'Donald, in a vessel on the navigable river Thames.
ANGUS M'DONALD . I belong to the Dreadnought Hospital ship. The prisoner slept next to me in my cabin—in the afternoon of the I5th of October I had a purse containing two half-crowns, 15 s., and the French pieces—I put them into my jacket pocket, under my head, when I went to bed—at half-past seven o'clock next morning I missed them—the boatswain found this money on the prisoner.
JOHN LORD. I am boatswain. After this complaint about the money. I spoke to the prisoner—he said the money belonged to him—I found 8 s., one five-franc piece, two single francs, and some coppers on him—I took him before the captain, and then he said he picked it up under the hammock.
JOHN BOVIS ( police-constable R 139.) I took the prisoner—he said, "It is done, and it can't be helped."
Prisoner's Defence. When I turned out it was under my hammock; and it is a rule when anything is found to take it to the captain, but we cannot see him till nine o'clock.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Four Months.

3054. WILLIAM DAVIE was indicted for stealing, on the 2nd of October, 1 watch, value 1 l.; 1 ring, value 1 l. 10 s.; 1 purse, value 6 d.; 1 half-sovereign, and 1 5 l. Bank-note; the property of George Webb : and MARY CRISP, for feloniously receiving the said 5 l. note, well knowing it to have been stolen.
MR. DOANE. conducted the Prosecution.
LIEUT. GEORGE WEBB . I am in the Royal Marines, and am quartered at Woolwich. Davie was in the service of a brother officer—on Sunday evening, the 2nd of October, I returned from dinner—Davie came up to me in the passage, and said a gentleman had been into my room inquiring for me; that he had stopped some time, then said he could not stop any longer, he would call again in the morning, and he had been gone half an hour—I asked his name—he gave me some name, which I have forgotten—next morning I missed my watch, ring, and a 5 l. note—I gave information to the police—this is the note—I have not found the watch and ring.
Davie. He was asked if he recollected the number of the note; he said, "No;" but when he got home he said he did. Witness. I knew the number, and it has the initials "J. B." on it.
JAMES PARRY ( police-sergeant R 8.) In consequence of information I went to the prisoner's lodgings, in High-street, Woolwich—I went into a room, and found Crisp and a marine, and another woman—the others left, and I said to Crisp, "What is your name?"—she said "Crisp"—I said, "I
suppose you have heard Davie is in trouble; what did he give you last night?"—she said, "Nothing"—I said, "What, this morning?"—she said "Nothing"—I moved the mattress and bed off, and saw five or six pieces of curled paper—she seized them—I caught her hand, forced it open, and found this 5 l. note in it—I said to the prosecutor, "What is the number of your note?"—he said, "76116, and 'J. B.' on it"—I said, "There it is"—I took Davie afterwards—he was told by the adjutant of the charge—I said it was a bad job for the poor woman—he said, "She is innocent; when I gave it to her I told her it would be of some use to her by and by."
Davie's Defence. A gentleman called, and asked if Mr. Webb was there; I said no; the door was open: he went in, and remained a quarter of an hour, and then went down.
DAVIE — GUILTY . Aged 30.— Transported for Seven Years.
CRISP — NOT GUILLY .

3055. THOMAS HOBBINS was indicted for stealing, on the 22nd of October, 1 pair of trowsers, value 1 l. 10 s., the goods of William Willans Dixon.
WILLIAM WILLANS DIXON . I live at Woolwich. On Sunday morning last I missed a pair of trowsers from my bed-room—I had placed them there on the Wednesday and had not taken any notice of them afterwards—these are them—the prisoner lodged within about twenty yards of me.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON Q. DO you know him? A. Yes—he is in the Marines—I hear he has had a good character—I have no wish to press against him.
ANN BOXER . I met the prisoner in the street—he touched me on the shoulder, and asked me to do him a favour, to pawn these trowsers for him—I asked him if it was anything regimental—he said, "No"—I pawned them for him—I never saw him before.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you pawn property for strangers? A. I had no doubt they were his own—I pawned them in my own name—I told the pawnbroker I was sent with them—I did not tell him I lived in High-street, he put that on the duplicate of his own accord, because I had lived there, and I have used his shop for four or five years.
SARAH STILL . I was passing Mr. Booth's, the pawnbroker's door, and saw the prisoner standing outside—he told me to go in and see if the man was writing out the duplicate for the woman—I went in and he was (I had seen Boxer go into the shop)—when she came out I saw her deliver some silver and a ticket into his hand.
WILLIAM LEACH . I took in these trowsers from Boxer.
GEORGE HARRIS . I took the prisoner—he said, "I know what you want, for God's sake do not say anything about it"—I said, "You are charged with stealing a pair of trowsers of Mr. Dixon, and I must search you"—he said, "Pray let me go up stairs"—he was going to put his hand to his mouth—I seized it, and he put this duplicate of the trowsers out of his mouth into his hand.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know what character he has borne.? A. I understand he has been seventeen years in the regiment, and has borne a very good character—I think the regiment will take him back.
GUILTY .— Confined Six Days.

3056. WILLIAM SMITH was indicted for wilful and corrupt perjury.
