3057. MICHAEL DONOVAN was indicted for feloniously assaulting Thomas Philip Davis on the 18th of October, putting him in fear, and taking from his person, and against his will, 16 shillings and 6 sixpences, his monies; and immediately before, at the time of, and after the said robbery, beating, striking, and using other personal violence towards him.
THOMAS PHILIP DAVIS . I live in Kent-street, Borough. On the 18th of October, after twelve o'clock at night, I was going along Kent-street—I was a little in liquor—the prisoner tripped me up and threw me in the kennel—be stooped down, but I did not observe him take the money—it was in my waistcoat pocket—my coat was open—he left me instantaneously, and when I got up I, missed my money and likewise the pad of my wooden leg—I had 18 s. or 19 s. in shillings and sixpences—I have not the least doubt the prisoner is the person—he did nothing to me while I was on the ground—he left me directly—he stooped down—I afterwards gave the prisoner into custody in consequence of something told me by the witness Barrett—I am certain he is the man—I had never seen him before to the best of my knowledge—I did not notice out of which house he came—it was quite dark, but it was light enough to see him—there was a lamp not far off.
MARGARET BARRETT . I am single, and lodge in Kent-street, in the same house as the prisoner. On Tuesday night, the 18th of October, after eleven o'clock, I heard the prisoner ask the deputy who takes care of the house if she would trust him for that night's lodging—she said "No," and he walked out—he said he had got no money—I went to bed about twelve o'clock—he came in between one and two, and slept in the same room as I did—next morning, about eleven o'clock, I saw him with a pad in his hand—he went out and brought in a young woman with him, and took a lodging for her—I saw them go out together and they came in with a new pair of shoes, a hat, a new pair of stockings, a pair of trowsers, and a coat—he had gone out in an old jacket and cap about nine o'clock—on the Friday night I saw him at the door in his shirt sleeves talking to the young woman—the prosecutor, his wife, and child passed by the door and the prisoner began to laugh at him, and said that was the man he had knocked down in the kennel off the footpath, and that he took the pad from under his knee, that he might not follow him—the woman pushed him into the house and told him to hold his tongue, as he did not know who might be listening to him—I was afterwards going up the same side of the way and met the prosecutor and his wife—I spoke to him, and he gave me into the custody of the police—I went to the house—the prisoner was having his supper with the young woman, and the policeman took him into custody—the pad has not been found.
Prisoner. Q. Have you not said you should get money enough by this to send you back to Ireland? A. Never—I do not want to go to Ireland—your brother and others say they will have my life—they are all robbers at this house—I have left it—the woman of the house receives
every bit of robbery that they bring in and locks it up—the landlord bought a side of bacon of you for 1 s. 9 d., which you stole—I did not sell it—saw the landlord buy it of you in the parlour—you lodged in the house from Sunday to Friday.
COURT. Q. How came he to sleep in the same room as you? A. There were ten or eleven beds in the room—I slept on one side of the room, and he on the other side with a young woman.
JOHN MOIR ( police-constable M 207.) The prosecutor gave Barrett into my charge—on her statement of the transaction, I went with her to a lodging-house, in Kent-street—she went in, came out, and gave me a description of the prisoner, and the part of the room he was sitting in—I went in and took him into custody—I brought him out—there was very little light where he stood, and the prosecutor said he thought he was not the man, but afterwards, when I took him into the house, where there was more light, he identified him, and gave him in charge—he made no answer to it, but said he thought he was taken for a robbery, which occurred with some girls, a few nights previous—the prosecutor pointed out to me the spot where he was knocked down—it was not more than eight or ten yards from the house where I took the prisoner—there is a lamp directly in front of the house—there was sufficient light to recognize any one—more so than where the prisoner stood when the prosecutor could not identify him—the light from the lodging-house window throws a better light a little way off than close to it, and there is a gas light to a public-house adjoining—when the prosecutor expressed a doubt, I said, "Come into the house and look round, there are plenty of them here"—he came in with me and looked round, and after scrutinizing the prisoner more, he said he was the man.
Prisoner, He did not; he said it was a man a head taller than me that robbed him. Witness. Not after he saw you in the house—Barrett told me that she had seen the pad in the prisoner's hand.
THOMAS PHILIP DAVIS re-examined. Barrett told me she had seen the pad, when she first spoke to me of the man that robbed me—I told her I had lost one, but not before she told me she had seen it—I had not talked to anybody about losing it—the prisoner was by my side when he trippd me up—I saw his face when he stooped down—I am sure I had my money about me at the time I was knocked down—I had seen it safe about ten minutes before—I had had no fall before I was knocked down to the best of my knowledge—I have no recollection of falling—I am certain the prisoner is the man that knocked me down—I said at first that it was a taller man, because it was dark there, but when they took him into the light I knew him—the house where the prisoner was taken is about six or sewn doors from where I was robbed—he was dressed very shabbily when he knocked me down—it was not far from a lamp, and a few shops were open—I have often been robbed before—I have not charged several people with robbing me—I have let them go—I charged a man that was tried many years ago in the New Court, and he was transported for fourteen years I believe—I never charged any one but the prisoner with this robbery—a little while ago I lost a 30 l. Bank of England note, some sovereigns, and a Bank Dividend Warrant—I went to the station about that—that is the only time—I have 1300 l. in the Three per Cents., and have a good bit of money at times—I take very little care of it.
Prisoner's Defence. I know nothing of the robbery; I was in bed at
the time with my brother, and the landlady of the house could prove it; the prosecutor could not recognize me, he said it was a man a head taller that robbed him, but the sergeant forced him to give me in charge; and when he came outside he said I was the man; he was in liquor then; Barrett speaks against me because the landlady would not trust her with a lodging that night.
TAYLOR ( police-inspector B.) I know the prosecutor—I believe him to be a very drunken character—he has made, I should say, two or three different charges against people for robbing him, and they have fallen to the ground; he refused to charge them at the station—I think it has been done in a drunken frolic.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Justice Erskine.

3059. GEORGE MOULDEN and JAMES KELLY were indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Caroline Banks, on the 27th of September, at St. Paul, Deptford, and stealing therein 1 basket, value. 1 s.; 12 spoons, value 7 l.; 11 forks, value 6 l.; 2 knife rests, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 11 knives, value 1 l. 10 s.; her property.
HANNAH SALMON . I am servant to Caroline Banks, of No. 2, Staffordshire-place, Old Kent-road, in the parish of St. Paul, Deptford. On the 27th of September, about nine o'clock in the morning, I went up stairs, leaving the plate basket in the kitchen cupboard, and the kitchen window down, but not fastened—I came down in about twenty minutes—the kitchen window was then wide open, and the plate basket gone—it contained the articles stated—I saw the property again in the course of the day at Union Hall.
FRANCIS BEAVER . I am a greengrocer, and live in Mason-street. On the 27th of September, between nine and ten o'clock in the morning, I was at my door, loading my cart, and saw the two prisoners go by, walking side by side, Moulden had a basket on his arm with a cloth over it—as they passed the cart, the cloth blew off, and I saw that the basket contained plate—I told the witness Brown to go and fetch a policeman—they must have heard me, for they walked on pretty sharp—Brown followed them up the road.
JOHN BROWN . I live in Mason-street. On the 27th of September, between nine and ten o'clock, Mr. Beaver called my attention to the two prisoners—Moulden had a basket with him—I could not see what was in it—they went over a field, towards London—I followed, and when I got into the Kent-road I was before them—I walked towards London, and looked out for a policeman—they followed me a short distance—when they came to the canal-bridge they met two more boys, who joined them—I continued walking till I got to the Green Man—I there saw a constable, and told him what I had observed, and saw him take the two prisoners and one of the others into custody.
JOHN WHITLAMB ( police-constable M 89.) I was on duty on the 27th of September, in the Old Kent-road—In consequence of what Brown said to me I apprehended Moulden—he was carrying this basket of plate in his
right hand, covered with this handkerchief, and a stick in his left hand, walking towards London—I asked what he had got there—he said what he had got was his own, that he had found it, and I had nothing to do with it.
Moulden. I did not say it was my own—I said I picked it up. Witness. He said what I have stated—he resisted, knocked the basket out of my hand, and ran away a short distance—I pursued and caught him again.
WILLIAM FENSOM ( police-constable M 204.) I was on duty in the Old Kent-road on the 27th of September, and saw the two prisoners and another boy coming up together towards the gate—when they came near the gate Kelly and the other boy crossed over the road and ran away, and Moulden went up towards the gate—this was a mile or more from Mason-street—I ran after Kelly and the others, and caught them—I asked what they ran for—they made no answer—I took them to the station—Kelly there said that he and Moulden went into a field together, and there found the property, along with a knife-box and some knives, which they left behind them—that he and Moulden were in company together, and the other boy had nothing to do with it—no knives were found.
HANNAH SALMON ( re-examined.) These are Miss Bank's property—they are worth 15 l.—she lives about twenty yards from Mason-street—the area-gate was left unlocked, and they came in that way, down theara steps—the initials "H. B." are on the plate—it belonged to Miss Bank's father—I have been in the habit of using them, and this is the basket in which they were lost.
Moulden's Defence. I found them.
Kelly's Defence. We were going to Deptford dock-yard to get a shlip—I saw a parcel lying in the corner of the field covered with an apron—I called Moulden—he uncovered it, and said, "I will take them home, they will be handy—you can carry this basket and the knives"—I said I should not carry any of them, and left them there.
MOULDEN *— GUILTY . Aged 15.
KELLY *— GUILTY . Aged 17.
Transported for Ten Years.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18421024-3060]
3060. GEORGE BENTLIFFE was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of October, 1 coat, value 5 l., the goods of John Smith; and that be had been before convicted of felony.
JOHN DARTNALL . I am coachman to Mr. John Smith. On the 8th of October my coat was on the box of my master's carriage, in the London-road—about twenty minutes to seven in the evening I had occasion to get off the box to buy some glass, and left my coat quite safe—I got on my box again, in about two minutes, and did not miss it till I got into Hart-street, Long Acre—this is it.
Prisoner. He said at the office, that he did not know whether it dropped or not. Witness. I am certain I never dropped it—it could not have dropped off the box.
JAMES BROOK . I am a policeman. I was on duty in the Waterloo-road at a quarter before seven o'clock on Saturday evening, 8th of October, and saw the prisoner with this coat on his back—he crossed the road walkmg—I asked where he got it from—he made no answer—I took him in charge.
Prisoner's Defence. I saw the coat lying in the road, took it up, and put it on, and was going to take it to the station.
JAMES CUDDY . I am a policeman. I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from the office of the clerk of the Peace for Surrey—( read.) I am certain he is the man—I was present at his trial.

3062. JANE BOWLER was indicted for the wilful murder of Joseph Bowler.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES COULTHRED . I am a surgeon in the Navy, and reside in Meltonterrace, Southwark-bridge-road—I attended the deceased several times during his life—I did so about six years ago, and have attended the family many years—on Saturday, the 17th of September, about ten o'clock, I was called in—he was then dead—he had been dead, I think, about au hour—a spot on the floor was pointed out to me—there were faces and a good deal of mucus on it, by the side of the bed—he was on the bed when I first saw him—(I had attended the prisoner in her confinement, between two and three months ago, I am not quite sure)—I opened the body of the deceased on the Monday—the cavity of the thorax was quite healthy, and all the viscera—the stomach was opened, and a portion of white powder was seen at the bottom of it, about two inches in length, and an inch in breadth, and a good deal of fluid—the intestines were all empty—the kidneys were larger than they should be—all the rest of the viscera were healthy—we took home the contents of the stomach, and examined it by analysis—I examined it by different tests—it turned out to be arsenic—I should suppose, there must have been about twenty grains of white oxide, commonly called arsenious acid—it is generally called white arsenic—if you purchase it in the shops it is called arsenic—I should say the deceased must have suffered very much indeed, they described him to be in a fit when they called me—I should think, from the appearances, the poison must have been taken into the stomach about six or seven hours—I consider twelve hours to be the extent of time within which it must have been administered—I think he would have died in less than that; but I have given the extent—I found some castor-oil in the stomach—I attribute the death of the deceased to the arsenic—I had said to the prisoner that I thought it would be proper to have him opened, they had better send for the beadle; and she expressed a wish to have him opened—she said that he had eaten apple-pudding on the Wednesday previous to the Saturday, and that he went into the yard and immediately cast it up again—she gave me what she said was some of the crust of the pudding—it was analysed, but there was no arsenic in
that—if there had been arsenic in the pudding taken on Wednesday I should have supposed it had been all cast up again—the people in the house told me he had been ill from the Wednesday till the Saturday—Mrs. Bowler said nothing to me about that—all that I believe she said to me was, that she wished to have him opened—I do not recollect anything more—I was in my shop on the Thursday morning before, at nine o'clock—I did not see her there then—I did not sell her any tincture of rhubarb that morning—I did not give her any advice—I had not seen her from the time I left her when she was confined.
Cross-examined by MR. BODKIN Q. Are you a member of the College of Surgeons? A. No—I am a surgeon of the Navy—I attended the prisoner in both her confinements—the last was between two and three month ago—her two children are living—she is in my debt for my attendance on both occasions—she has not paid me the expenses of her first or her last confinement—she always paid me the fee, but not the bill for medicine, on either occasion—they have been sent in—before she was confined she said, "I will call some day and pay your bill"—I never asked her for it—I had sent in the bill, and she excused herself on one occasion for not paying the last—I took the contents of the stomach home, and subjected them to the usual tests—I reduced the arsenic into the metallic form, by the black flux—I have no doubt at all the stomach contained arsenic—I also tried it by Marks' apparatus—I was assisted in making the analysis by my assistant Wevell—he is not here—he was before the Coroner, and examined—he serves customers who come to our shop—the crust of pudding which was given me, was not what was rejected by the deceased's stomach on Wednesday—I had no means or opportunity of examining that—one of the earliest symptoms following taking of arsenic is violent sickness, and it is frequently cast up, if it is a largish quantity, with scarcely any injury—I think arsenic was taken on the Wednesday, and rejected from the stomach—I should say it might be incorporated in the fruit of the pudding, and not in the crust; or it might be taken independently, and food being taken at the same time, would be thrown up, by the action of the poison on the stomach—castor oil is a very powerful counter-irritant—it is no antidote—it would certainly be likely to lessen the acrimony of the poison, and carry it off the stomach, by its purgative qualities—it is impossible to say at what time the arsenic might have been taken—I should say twelve hours is the extent—I do not remember stating, shortly after this matter occurred, that it was impossible to tell that it might not have been taken, some days—I am sure I did not say, "There is nothing in its not having acted immediately, in a hot climate; in the East Indies a man might hare arsenic in his stomach six days"—I said nothing of the sort—I never mentioned the East Indies—the prisoner did not, in the conversation I had with her on the Saturday, tell me that she got the rhubarb from my assistant—at the time of the Coroner's inquest she said that he had served her—I do not know what day that was—I was not in Court at the time.
MR. PAYNE. Q. How much did they owe you for medicine? A. Not more than 2 l. or 3 l.—I should not have objected to go if I had been sent for—if the quantity of poison I found in the stomach had been administered on the Wednesday, it would have occasioned death earlier than the Saturday.
SARAH MORLEY . At the time I was examined I lived in Union-street, Borough; I knew the deceased by living in the same house for seventeen weeks. On the Sunday before the Saturday on which he died, the prisoner was in my room, and our conversation began about the newspaper—I told her a lady and gentleman and their servant had been poisoned with arsenic, by their daughter—she asked me whether I knew how they sold it, I whether in liquid or powder—nothing was said about the name it was called by—I saw the deceased on the morning he died—I cannot tell what time, it was before dinner—he was sick when 1 first went into the room—he brought something up, but what I do not know—the prisoner was there at that time—she said he brought up every thing she gave him—I asked her why she did not have a doctor—she said, "We owe money"—I said, "Why, there are parish doctors and dispensary doctors; why not have a doctor?"—the deceased said, "Why not me have a doctor?"—she said, "You don't want to be physicked and tortured about"—he had his hand on his chest—I did not hear him complain of anything at that time—on the Monday after the Saturday on which he died I was conversing with Mrs. Parfitt, whom Mrs. Bowler calls her mother—Mrs. Bowler was present—I told her that he had said, "Why not have a docto?"—she said, "He did not say so; and if you say that, you will hang me, for you will be called on the Jury"—on the Sunday morning the day after his death, I was cleaning the room, after laying him out, and brought a half-pint mug off the drawers' in the room, and took it into the kitchen—there was some tea in it—I emptied it in the yard, on the stones by the water-closet—I saw a sort of sediment in the bottom of the mug, of a dirty white, like magnesia—I then took the mug up stain to my husband, to show him—he told me to take it down and place it in the kitchen, and I did so—I did not wash it out—I never saw it afterwards—a man named Dunster lodged in the house—Mrs. Bowler told me at one time, just before Mr. Bowler's death, that if he (Dunster) got married to the young woman be was paying bis addresses to, she would destroy their happiness—on one occasion, previous to Mr. Bowler's death, she told me that Dunster would stop five years for her—I never saw her and Dunster go out together.
Cross-examined. Q. The prisoner was of a very cheerful and lively disposition, was she not? A. Yes—I cannot say whether the observations about Dunster were made in a joking way—she was not joking at the time, she was speaking very serious—I did not attach any importance to them—I had no conversation with her husband about it—I cannot say whether the prisoner was left an orphan, and brought up by Mr. Parfitt—I believe she was left there for the purpose of looking after the lodgers—I have had no conversation at all about this matter with any one—I am a stranger in the neighbourhood—I know Mrs. Clark and Mrs. Jones by coming on the Jury—I have had no conversation with either of them about this matter at any time—I had not seen them till I came here—we came together as witnesses—we have had no conversation as to what we were to say—I cannot remember any conversational all, either before we went before the Coroner, or since—my husband had read in the newspaper about the lady and gentleman being poisoned, and had told me of it—the newspaper was not before me—I told the prisoner what my husband had told me—I said it was a shocking thing—I do not remember her saying so, or saying anything at all about it—she said it was a very shocking
thing—I do not remember saying that the doctors would not sell articles of that sort—she said she had often known oxalic acid used to clean straw bonnets, that she had often fetched it for her sister—I never heard the deceased complain further than putting his hand on his chest—the nurse, named Inwood, suggested that a blister would be a good thing for him—it was the nurse that nursed her, not him—I do not know whether she was there assisting the deceased or not—I said a blister was a very safe thing, and I believe the prisoner said, "I won't have him tortured with a blister"—she said he was labouring under a bowel complaint—that was what was considered to be his ailment—she said his chest was bad, and that was why I recommended a blister—I was in the bed-room, standing by the bed-side—I do not hear very distinctly—on the Monday, when I was telling Mrs. Parfit that the deceased had wished to have a doctor, the prisoner said he did not say it—I said I knew he did say so, because I heard him—she did not say that I was mistaken, being deaf, nor, "If you did hear him, why did not you fetch one?" or anything to that effect—she said, "If you say that on the jury, it will hang me"—she did not say, after that, "Why, you are so spiteful, you would hang me if you could," or any thing of that sort—Mrs. Parfit was there—I did not hear her say so—I was not in and out of the deceased's room during the last day of his illness—I only saw him twice during his illness—I could go in when I liked—after his death I was left in full possession of the room, and did what was necessary to the body—Mrs. Jones and I laid him out and washed him, and I cleaned the room out—the prisoner asked me to do so—she was then in the parlour, the adjoining room—the mug was left on the drawers, and could be seen by anybody—there was a tea-cup by the side of it.
MR. PAYNE Q. Had you any spite against the prisoner? A. Not at all—I had no quarrel with her before her husband's death—I have no spite against her now—I am very sorry for her.
JAMES THOMAS . I live in Wellington-place, Friar-street, Blackfriars-road. I knew the deceased for four years, his name was Joseph Bowler—he was a Windsor chair-maker—I last saw him alive on the Wednesday morning about ten o'clock at the shop—I worked for the same master—he seemed much the lame as usual then—I heard of his illness—I inquired of the prisoner on Friday at dinner-time how he was—she said he was very ill in bed—on Saturday evening, near ten o'clock, I was at the corner of Union-street—the prisoner came to me, took hold of my hand, and said, Thomas, come to my place, Joe is dead"—she seemed very much excited and distressed—I went to the house, and found the deceased on the floor the green-grocer next door had got him under the arm, endeavouring to lift him up—I assisted in lifting him on the bed—the prisoner was sitting crying on the sofa in the next room—I observed a sort of dark phlegm protruding from his nose and mouth—I saw no glass or phial near him the prisoner told me on the Saturday night, that she had given him some brandy, and had purchased three pennyworth of tincture of rhubarb from Mr. Coulthred's shop, and given him that—she said she had made a bason of gruel for Mr. Dunster, a lodger, and was just going to make one for her husband.
CHARLOTTE TROTT . I am a widow, and live at No. 23, Union-street, next door to where the deceased lived. I knew him well for some years—the prisoner and he had been married about six years—the prisoner told me on the Thursday of his being ill—on Friday I met her in Union-street
about four o'clock in the afternoon—she had a cup, and was coming in a direction from the Blackfriars-road—I saw a liquid in the cup—I smelt it, and considered it was rhubarb—I asked her if she was going to give it to her husband—she said, "Yes"—I asked her if Coulthred said it was a good thing—she said, "Yes"—I saw her on the Saturday afternoon, and asked how he was—she said he was very ill—I asked if he was able to go to work—she said, "Oh dear no, he is not able to get up"—on the Saturday night I was called in by Dunster, the lodger—I went immediately, and saw the deceased laying dead on the floor—he was about twenty-nine years of age.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you married a son of Mrs. Trott? A. No, Mr. Benjamin Trott is my husband's brother—I have known the prisoner pretty well all her life—I do not know that she was left an orphan—I knew her by being there with Mrs. Parfitt, a relation of mine, who brought her up from her infancy, and treated her as her own child—she has been with the family ever since—she and her husband were placed in this house by Mrs. Parfitt—it was part of her duty to attend to the lodgers, in consequence of which they lived rent free—they married very young—it was her own choice, and her friends were all agreeable, I believe—he was a man of a gloomy disposition, and of very retired habits—he frequently passed my house, but we merely passed the compliments of the day, "Good morning, a fine day," and so on—we never associated further—I was not in his company of late, and do not know whether that manner had increased of late—I do not know of my own knowledge of any attempt he has made to destroy himself—I have heard so.
MR. PAYNE Q. How long ago did you hear of any thing of the sort? A. last week—I only heard of it then.
THOMAS LEADBEATER . I am a chair-maker, and live in the Borough-road. The deceased was in my employment—he worked for me last on the Wednesday before he died, the 17th of September—he did not come to work after that—I saw him on Wednesday morning—he had been in my employ eighteen or twenty months this time—I have known him much longer, he was apprenticed to me seventeen years ago—on one or two occasions he appeared rather dull, that was all—I did not see him after Wednesday.
HARRIET JONES . I am the wife of Essex Jones, a chair-maker, and live in the Borough-road—I knew the deceased. On Wednesday afternoon the prisoner told me he was taken very ill, with a violent vomiting, after eating some apple-pudding—I sent my son to inquire after him from day to day—I saw him on Wednesday when he went to his dinner, and saw him no more till he was dead—I was present when he was taken off the floor—I saw his forehead and nose bruised—the prisoner said, in the course of the Saturday night, that she wished him to be opened to satisfy the world, as he died so suddenly—I was there till Sunday morning—I washed out a cup of castor-oil and peppermint-water—there was nothing in that—I saw no other cup in the room.
Cross-examined. Q. There was an immediate talk about a Coroner's inquest on him? A. Yes, they said they expected there would be an inquest.
ESSEX JONES . I am the husband of the last witness. The day before the deceased died I saw the prisoner in the Blackfriars-road, opposite the door of a chemist's shop, nearly opposite the end of Webber-street—I do
not know the name of the person that keeps the shop—I asked bow Mr. Bowler was—she said he was a little better, and she thought perhaps he would be able to come to his work on the Monday following—on the Monday after the Saturday on which he died, I asked if she had called it the chemist's on Friday to get any thing to compose him—she said no she had not been there at all, she had been in the road to purchase some soap—she said, in the presence of some other people, that she should have no objection whatever to the body being opened, it would then satisfy the public whether he had poisoned himself or not—she afterwards said the should not like him opened; in fact, he should not be opened unless she gave her consent—she asked me if, in the event of the body being opened, the doctor could ascertain whether there was poison in the stomach or not—I said decidedly he could—that was about the latter part of the conversation.
COURT. Q. She did not ultimately object to his being opened? A. Not at all—she said she thought it was quite necessary she should be asked, being his wife—she did not say she would refuse if she was asked—she said it was necessary if they came to do it, that they should have her consent.
MARY MUSGRAVE . I live in Pepper-street. I was employed to none Mrs. Bowler—on the Saturday on which her husband died I was in the house at one o'clock—I was not there at all on the Wednesday, Thursday, or Friday—he appeared very ill—he was in great pain at the pit of his stomach, and was sick—the prisoner was there—I fetched some castor oil with a little peppermint water, and gave it to Mrs. Bowler, and saw her go into the bed-room—I did not go in myself at that time—I saw him again about half-past four o'clock the same day—he was very much convulsed then—the prisoner was there—he never spoke at all at that time, only shook his head—I staid till eight—I did not see him after—I was cleaning and doing my business in the house—I was not in his room—Mrs. Bowler was in the parlour—nobody was with him then—I asked her when I went away, between seven and eight, how he was—she said he was dosing—I went up at eleven, and found him dead—I saw the prisoner then—she was very much affected at that time.
Q. On what terms did they appear to live? A. There was never hardly any words between them—more of silent contempt, sulky like, to one another—they had no words before anybody.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you attend her in both confinements? A. No, only in the last—she has attended to her two children herself—she had no means of support but her husband's earnings—she went into her husband's room both times when I went in—I had my business to do—she went for the castor oil, and took it into his room—they lived comfortably together, as far as I observed—I was there for a month—during the lying-in he came home at night regularly.
MR. PAYNE. Q. They did not appear to take a great deal of notice of each other? A. Not a great deal.
COURT. Q. How many other lodgers were in the house? A. Four, with Mr. Dunster, besides the Bowlers—six altogether—one was Mrs. Morley—I forget the other two—they were up stairs on the second pair—one was a widow and two children—the other was a man and his wife—three women lived in the house—that was at the time I was nurse, when
the last baby was born—all these people were not living there at the time of his death—there were two rooms empty.
SARAH CLARK . I am the wife of Henry Clark, a chair-maker, of No. 48, Belvidere-place, Borough-road. I knew the deceased—on the Monday after the Saturday on which he died, Isaw Mrs. Bowler, when I came back from the inquest-room, after the inquest had been adjourned—I think it was after the third meeting—there were three Mondayi and one Friday—I attended at three meetings—I did not go on the first Monday—I was at the house all the week after he died—the first time I went there was on the Monday night—I had no conversation with her then—on the next Monday I had no conversation with her—I bad only one conversation with her—that was on the Monday before the Friday on which the inquest closed—I do not know the day of the month—as I returned from the Jury-room I called at Mrs. Bowler's—Mrs. Parfitt was there, and she said in the prisoner's presence, "Well, Mrs. Clark, how do you get on to night?"—I said it was postponed till another night—Mrs. Bowler burst out laughing—I said, "Do not laugh, Jane? it is no laughing matter"—she laughed, and said, "Well, if I laugh it it not right, and if I cry it is not right, and what am I to do?"—Mr. Dunster was present at the time—he turned round, and said, "Well, Jane, you ought to know better"—she immediately turned round, and said, "Now, Dunster, you had better hold your tongue, or by G—I will open, and say something that you will not like"—he said, "Well, Jane, I stand manfully before you, and if you have anything to say openly, say it; I will most happily hear you, with the greatest of pleasure"—she said, "I have trusted to one, and no doubt I shall get over it; I am in it, and must get out of it in the best manner I can; there is one thing, they cannot hang me, on account of my children"—she said that partly in a serious and partly in a jocular way—nothing more particular passed that night—we had supper, and went to bed—I remained there all night—next morning we were at breakfast, and the prisoner said, "Well, if he has been poisoned, as it seems he has, he must have done it himself"—she laughed about it—she was partly jocular, and partly serious, middling.
Cross-examined. Q. Did she not at this time say, "I am quite innocent, but they are all against me, and wicked enough to hang me if they can?" A. I never heard anything of the sort—I can repeat what passed from beginning to end, from the time I was there first—I was asked from the Monday at the inquest—I have not been asked as to the evidence I gave first.
MR. PAYNE. Q. I asked you what passed? A. You asked me the last part instead of the first—I heard Mr. Bowler was bad on Saturday night—I did not go till the first Monday after his death, when I came from my work—there was not much conversation then—I remained there till the doctor came—I was at the opening of the body—I heard that he died by poison—I remained there all night, during the week, and I think on the Wednesday, Mrs. Bowler said it was a shocking thing, but thank God she had not done it, that nothing had passed through his body except what she had given him, but poison she had not—I remained there till the Saturday, and she still said that nothing had passed through his body except what she had given him herself.
MR. BODKIN Q. Did she not also say that she was very glad his body had been opened? A. Not to me—she said it in my hearing—I
had known her husband nine or ten years, and Dunster this last twelve months, but never saw him much—I have seen him at Mrs. Bowler's—I heard my husband say something about the deceased attempting to destroy himself—I was with my husband, the deceased, and the prisoner, coming home from Mr. Bowler's mother's at Lambeth, and I heard my husband say that he had attempted to throw himself into the canal—I did not see him—it is about five years ago—he left our party and ran from the door—I saw him with my husband at a public-house door—I and Mrs. Bowler were at his mother's door—we met and came away together, and were to have gone home together—they left me and Mrs. Bowler behind some distance—my husband went after Mr. Bowler—I did not know what they were on—he went down the street—I saw Bowler go away down the street, and my husband after him—I do not know whether there is a canal near there—I never saw one—I have bees in that neighbourhood I think about twice in my life—I know there is some water by the Bishop's Palace, where I went with Mrs. Bowler to church once—it is the river I suppose—Bowler ran down the street—I do not know whether it would lead him to that water—I heard so—I do not know at all—I was not there—my husband followed him quickly, and brought him back—neither of them were wet—my husband told me what Bowler was going to do—he was not examined before the Coroner—he is here now.
MR. PATNE Q. Was he examined before the Grand Jury? A. No—he has not been examined as a witness at all—he was subpoanaed.
COURT. Q. On this Monday evening, when this talk was with the prisoner and Dunster, had the body been opened? A. Yes, and they said that he died through poison—I should think there was then a talk about who had done it—I did not notice anything of that.
JOHN PAGE . I am assistant to Messrs. Lowe and Hornblower, chemists, Blackfriars-road. I was examined before the Coroner, on the last occasion (on a Thursday, I think) about three weeks ago—I do not recollect the day of the month—I remember about three weeks before I was examined, a woman coming and asking me for arsenic—I did not give it her—the prisoner has since been pointed out to me—she came with the officer—my decided impression is that she is the person—that was my impression when she was brought to me, and it is so still—I would not undertake to swear positively to her, but my impression is still the same.
Cross-examined. Q. How long was it after this application to you that you were first shown her? A. Four or five days, I think, not more—about the middle of the next week—our shop is a place of good business—people come in and out constantly—she was brought to me by an officer—she was the only woman with him—he had been with me before—he had not told me he would bring her—he had inquired of me abort it—I did not expect him to bring her—he asked me if I should knot her if I was to see her, and on that he brought her—I did not say, on first seeing her, "I think I have seen you before, but you take me all of a nonplus," or anything of the kind—the words I used were, "I have seen your face before," not "I think I have seen your face before," but I will not swear it—the officer did not ask me whether I remembered serving her with any thing, or anything of the kind, nor did I say, "Why, I can't tell that"—the officer did not ask the question—she was only brought once—I do not remember the prisoner saying, "Look at me carefully"
or "stedfaatly," nothing of the sort—she said she had never been in the shop before—I cannot speak positively as to every part of her dress—when she was brought by the officer she had, I should say, a straw bonnet on, and a dark shawl—she was not in mourning—she was dressed in a dark dress and a straw bonnet—I remember having my attention called to her before the Coroner—I did not then say I could not speak to her accurately because she was in mourning—I said, "She is in mourning now"—I did not say I could not swear to her because her dress was different, nothing of the kind—I am quite positive I did not say, at any time, that I would not undertake to swear to her because her dress was different—this signature to the depositions is my handwriting—it was read over to me before I signed it—these last two lines are quite right—it is exactly what I said, but it is punctuated differently—( The witness's deposition being read, stated, " I would not undertake to swear to her, her dress is different, she is now in mourning")—that was in answer to a separate question—I told you just now that I said nothing about a doubt of her person in reference to her change of dress—I still adhere to that answer.
COURT. Q. You spoke of a doubt about her person, independently of her dress? A. The Coroner asked me if I was still of the same opinion, and I said I was—and separately I said, "but I see her dress is different; I see she is now in mourning"—I still say I will not undertake to swear to her, but I am still of the same opinion of her as I was before the Corner—I should say it was about three o'clock in the day that the application was made to me for the arsenic—I am not quite certain—I noticed nothing remarkable about the woman—she was in the shop but a very short time, just time enough to ask for arsenic—I refused her—she was not in the shop a moment—she asked me for a certain quantity, but I do not recollect the manner in which she asked for it—she was jast there time enough to say so much arsenic, and I said, "We do not sell it"—she went away immediately—we have not very often had applications for arsenic, not for small quantities—we sell an immense quantity in a larger way, to manufacturers, but we very seldom sell it in small quantities—now and then we do, to persons we know—we refuse selling it to persons we do not know—I cannot tell how many customers come to our house in the course of a day—perhaps near a hundred—I cannot say how many in an hour—sometimes more, sometimes less—there is not five minutes without a customer coming in.
JOSHUA ALLINGHAM . I am beadle of St. George's, Southwark. On Sunday evening I received information of the death of the deceased—I "went to the house on Monday morning—I saw the prisoner—Mr. Dunster was in the room—it is my duty to summon inquests—I asked how long he had been ill—she said he had left his work on the Wednesday, complaining of cold and pain in the bowels—I then asked when the death took place—she said on the Saturday night, about ten o'clock—I asked why, as he had been ill from the Wednesday, she had had no medical man to see him—she said that he had an objection to seeing a medical man—I asked how long she had left the room previous to finding him dead—(I asked that question to see if it was a case for an inquest to be held)—she said she had been absent from the room somewhere about half-an-hour, she could not be certain, that hearing a fall in the room she proceeded to the room, and found him on the floor—I then asked her what she had done—she said she immediately called assistance, and screamed out—I asked her
who that party was—she said it was Mr. Dunster, that she called him into the room, that he then left for the purpose of calling other assistance and fetched another person named Runn, and likewise Mrs. Trott, and Mr. Dunster then went and fetched Mr. Coulthred—I did not make any search in the room at that time—on the Tuesday morning I saw Mr. Coulthred, but not in the prisoner's presence—on the Tuesday evening the inquest sat—Mr. Coulthred having made the post mortem examination, it then came out that it was a case of poisoning, and I made further inquiries—I saw the prisoner on the Wednesday—she seemed to be very much affected—on inquiry I found that she had been out on the Friday, and I asked her for what purpose she went out on the Friday, and where she went to—she said she had been to the Blackfriars-road for the purpose of getting some tincture of rhubarb, for Mr. Bowler—I then inquired where she got it—she said at a chemist's shop, opposite Rowland Hill's chapel—she could not tell the name of the chemist, she did not remember it, but I have found out that it was Jones—she said that it was at a chemist's shop within two or three doors of the New-cut—I asked what they had bad for dinner on the Wednesday—she said apple-pudding—I enquired whether there was any portion of it left—she said there was—she fetched some of the crust for me, and I afterwards took it to Mr. Coulthred in a saucer—Mrs. Bowler went with me willingly to the different places—I asked her to change her dress, from the mourning dress to a coloured bonnet, and she did it willingly.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you take her to several chemists' shops in the neighbourhood? A. I did, among others to Mr. Jones—the cup, or mug, in which the rhubarb was said to have been fetched, was taken with her—Mr. Jones could not call her to mind at the time, but when I produced the cup he recollected that, and he then asked her what quantity she had—she said, "Three-penny worth"—he said, "How far would the three-penny worth come up to in the cup?"—she then pointed to a certain mark, and Mr. Jones immediately told the assistant to put so many grains of water in, and it corresponded exactly with what she said—this was on the 27th, I think, the Monday week after his death—I went to the house from time to time for the whole three weeks the Coroner's jury were sitting—I always found her at home, and most willing to accompany me wherever I suggested—on the same day that I took her to Jones I took ber to Hornblower's—I had been there before that day, and had seen the witness Page—I asked him if he thought he should be able to recognize her again—be said he thought he should—I intimated to him that I would bring her—I cannot positively say that I told him I would bring hw—I did not say any thing about when I would come—from the conversation he of course would expect me to bring her—she had a cotton gown on, and the bonnet that she said she was out with when she went to get the tincture of rhubarb on the Friday—she herself told me how she bad been dressed on the Friday—she took off her widow's cap and dress and put thw one on—a man named Lowring accompanied us to Mr. Page—she wai the only female—I remember Page saying, when he saw her, "I think I hive seen you before"—it was the first thing he said—I do not remember bw saying any thing about being taken rather at a nonplus —I then asked him whether that was the party that had applied to him for arsenic, and be said he had no doubt that she was—when I first went in, he said, "I have seen you before"—I will not be positive whether he said, "I think," or not—
the woman herself said nothing at all—I do not think she uttered a word—I do not think she denied being the person that had been there—I will not be positive as to that—I cannot charge my memory to say whether she made any observation, when he said he had no doubt of it—she stood some short distance from me—I remember something about her calling on him to look at her steadfastly and carefully—I will not be positive whether she denied being in the shop or not.
COURT. Q. What time of day was this? A. Between two and three in the afternoon, I believe—she and I went away together—I did not return to her house with her—I walked a short distance along Blackfdarsr road with her.
HENRY CLARK . I am the husband of Sarah Clark. MR. BODKIN. Q. DO you remember any occasion on which the deceased made an attempt on his life? A. Yes—I was with him at the time—it was between four and five years ago—I have known him ten or twelve years, and worked with him—I was with him and his brother-in-law at his mother's, at Lambeth—I forget his brother-in-law's name—he lives at Dartford—my wife and Mrs. Bowler were there—we were ail together—his brother-in-law came to the shop, and he went out to drink with him—we went from there to his mother's at Lambeth—I saw him outside the door, and he said, "I shall go and jump into the Thames"—he ran very hard from me, direct to the Thames—I ran very hard after him, and stopped him about twenty feet from the water—I said, " Joe, what are you at?"—he said, "I shall go and jump into the Thames, and drown myself"—I brought him away—my wife was there, but not when I stopped him—she was along with Mrs. Bowler—we were all five to gether in company outside when he started—we were, it might be, 400 yards from the Thames—I know of no attempt he made since that.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Had you been with him all the day? A. No—only three or four hours—nothing had happened to make him do this—he was very tipsy at the time—he did not get nearer the water than twenty feet—I think he would have gone into the Thames that night if I had not stopped him.
COURT Q. Was he melancholy, or dissatisfied about anything? A. No, only he was in liquor.
MR. BODKIN called the following witnesses.
ELIZABETH TROTT . I am the wife of a publican, who keeps the Nun's-head at Peckham—my mother's name is Parfitt—I have known the prisoner since a few years after she was born—my mother was acquainted with the prisoner's parents, and she took the prisoner at her father's death—she was then not quite seven months old—she apprenticed her to Mrs. Coxton, a milliner, and kept her until her marriage—she received a very good plain education, according to my mother's circumstances—she knew the deceased some years previous to her marriage, which took place six years ago in November—she and her husband came to live in my house in Union-street—they came there at my mother's request, to wait on her, consequence of her being very much crippled—my mother afterwards came to me for the benefit of her health, and the prisoner and her husband continued to reside there to take care of her house, in return for which they lived rent-free—it was occupied by various lodgers—Mr. Dunster was one of them—a niece of mine, named Charlotte Trott, is engaged to
Dunster, and has been so some months—I have known Dunster personally for ten or twelve years, but I have known there was such a person this twenty years—he is a first-cousin of my husband's—I knew the prisoner's husband—I never could form an idea of his disposition—he was very gloomy in my presence—I never exchanged half-a-dozen words with him at one time, and I have been with him for hours—he was so reserved in his manner, he never held any conversation with any of us—he was quite of a reserved gloomy habit, and, I should say, desponding—I never saw him merry or cheerful—I have known the prisoner and deceased for many years—they were not brought up together, only for a few years—they came together being inmates of one house.
COURT. Q. How long has Dunster been paying his addresses to your niece? A. Some months—I have known of it since May last, I cannot Bay how long before—I was present on the occasion of Mrs. Clark coming from the inquest, and saying that it was again postponed—that was after the first adjournment—Mrs. Clark stated some observation made by out of the Jury—the prisoner smiled, and my mother chided her for laughing at it—she said she did not consider it was a time for levity—the prisoner directly said if she laughed it was not right, if she cried it was not right, what was she to do?—she had cried at various times, but not that evening, and my mother had cheered her when she was low spirited, comforted her, and said, "Don't cry"—the prisoner said she considered people were very much prejudiced against her, and she did not know for what or for why—she said a great many would glory to see her hanged, but there was one above who knew all, and that was the one she would trust to—she is a person of a good and humane disposition—we have been brought up together as sisters—she was very open until her marriage, and then her circumstances had been so different, that her spirits have been curbed.
JOHN DUNSTER . I am twenty-eight years old, and am a traveller to a commercial house—I have lived in the house where Mr. and Mrs. Bowler lived, about eighteen months—I knew them very well for at least nine years before that—I have not been particularly intimate with them—I know the Trotts, and the other parts of the family—Mr. John Trolt has a niece named Charlotte—I have been paying attentions to her for six months and more, with the approbation of her own friends and mine-our intimacy continues down to the present time—the prisoner had not intimated to me at any time any displeasure on her part, to my attentions to Miss Trott—I remember the deceased attempting to destroy himself in Peckham-fields, twelve months ago last May—we had been to the Nun's Head, Peckham, and were coming home together—I was a little before him—his wife was there—we were coming over the bridge together, and he said he would throw himself into the b—canal—his wife entreated him not to do so, and to come away—he said he would not come away, he would not leave, he would drown himself—I assisted her in endeavouring to prevent it—I asked him why he did so—he said he was tired of his life—I thought at first it was a joke, but not afterwards—he seemed very determined—I succeeded with his wife in getting him away home—he was of a very sullen temper—he talked very little—on two or three occasions I had seen him in a room, in company with other person—I never observed any thing particular—he was generally very quit—I think he has remained in the room—I never particulary observed—he did not join in the conversation like other persons—I never thought that
he was depressed—he was very reserved—it appeared to me to be his general disposition—on one occasion, about seven months ago, he told me that he had been home to his dinner, and had nothing but bread and cheese, and had been home to his supper and had the same—he then talked of his bringing home money to provide different food, and he had not got that which he expected—he two or three times complained of great poverty—it was the duty of the prisoner to attend to me, among the other lodgers in the house—I have never said that she was to wait for me, or any thing intimating any intention on my part to marry her, or be acquainted with her improperly—there is not the slightest truth in it—I was about to leave the lodgings—I gave notice last May, at least I spoke to Mrs. Parfitt about leaving, because I did not like to live in the house, in consequence of Mrs. Bowler being negligent—I stated as the reason, that I thought she did not pay attention enough to the lodgers—I also told Mrs. Trott of my intention to leave, and complained of the prisoner at the reason, but she asked me to wait until after her confinement.
COURT. Q. Were any attentions paid by you to Mrs. Bowler? A. Not at all—there was no making love at any time—never anything of the kind between us.
MR. BODKIN. Q. I believe you were in the house at the time of the death occurring? A. Yes, Mrs. Bowler's screaming called me into the room—I went in, and found the deceased dead—Mrs. Bowler seemed almost in hysterics—she said nothing on my going in—I asked her what was the matter—she said that her poor dear Joe was dead—those were the words she used—within a minute or two I went for assistance, and brought in the neighbours within a minute of each other, and Mr. Coulthred also—I was present the day that Clark came in after the inquest was adjourned—she first laughed, and Mrs. Parfitt and Mrs. Trott called her to account for it, and she said they were as bad as other people until they were found out—I do not know what that bad reference to—I suppose it was merely said out of aggravation—no other cause that I am aware of—I told her I thought she was very ungrateful—she said if I did not hold my tongue, after the affair was settled, she would say something I did not like—I wished her to say what it was, and urged her several times—I did not hear her say that she trusted to one, or that she was in it, and must get out of it—I think she said at the same time that we would all hang her if we could—I asked what we should get by hanging her—I have heard since, that the death had been a good deal talked about in the neighbourhood—this was a fortnight after the inquest began—the women who have been here to-day as witnesses were in the habit of coming to the home—Mrs. Morley lived in the house—Mrs. Clark was there for a week—I was examined before the Coroner—I was not bound over as a witness for the prosecution.
MR. PAYNE Q. Was there any body besides yourself and Mrs. Bowler in the house when the man died? A. Nobody—I was in the front room at the time, with Mrs. Bowler—I should think we had been in the room together about twenty minutes—I had not seen the deceased from the previous Sunday till then—I did not see him in the course of that day—I heard that he was ill—I did not go to see him at all—I came home on Wednesday at tea-time, and slept there—I was in the house on Thursday and Friday morning and evening—I did not go to see the deceased the whole time he was ill—during the twenty minutes Mrs. Bowler and I were
in the parlour, I was eating a basin of gruel which she had prepared for me—I did not hear the man groan at all—I gave him nothing—I do not know why I said "Not to me," when you asked me if the prisoner had ever complained of my being about to marry somebody else—I had no reason for saying so—she never has said any thing to me about it—the front parour was my sitting room as well as that of other members of the house—Mrs. Bowler and me repeatedly went to the play together—her husband never went with us—it was by his wish that I took his wife to the theatre—I asked him first—I should think we went twenty times, and he used to stay at home—he used to go on other nights, not with us—we used to come home soon after the play was over, at twelve o'clock, perhaps, or half-past sometimes—we have sometimes gone to have a little refreshment after the play was over—we did not do so at all times—I did not complain of the prisoner's laughing, and say it was ungrateful of her—not of her laughing, but of her speaking to Mrs. Parfitt and Mrs. Trott in the way she did, in an impatient manner—she did not say, "You hold your tongue or I will open on you"—she said, if I did not hold my tongne after this affair was settled, she would say something I did not like.
MR. BODKIN Q. This room was the sitting-room, and one to which other lodgers had access? A. Not the lodgers, but the family, Mrs. Trott and Mrs. Parfitt—it was the place where I am accustomed to have any refreshment—it was her duty to bring it to me—she did so on that evening—I had no idea that the deceased was seriously ill—she said he was nothing more than poorly, and I understood that it was nothing more than the bowel complaint—this was in September—I do not remember the day—I do not know that there was a good deal of bowel complaint about then.
COURT. Q. How near were you to the room in which the deceased died? A. In the next room—she went out of the front room into tint room, and screamed, and I went in and found him dead.
GEORGE PARFIT . I live at the Nunhead, Peckham—my wife is a cripple, unable to come here. I have been acquainted with the prisoner from infancy—I brought her up as my own child—her father and mother died when she was six or seven months old—as long as I have known her she was a very open, generous young woman, of a candid disposition—she married Bowler with the consent of myself and wife—they were brought up together—he was apprenticed to Mr. King—they lived very comfortably together—he was a very sullen, stubborn young man—you never could get anything hardly out of him—he was gloomy as long as I have known him, which was the six years they were married—I knew him before, but not much.
COURT. Q. You cannot tell whether he was as gloomy before marriage? A. No, I do not know.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Was he always a man of that kind? A. Ever since I have known him to be married.
MR. PAYNE. Q. He was a man of very few words? A. Very few—he would sit two or three hours and not speak.
SUSAN EDWARDS . I live in Rye-lane, Peckham. I have been acquainted with the prisoner ever since she was two years old, to the present moment—I always considered her of a perfectly kind disposition, free-hearted, and good natured to the extreme—I knew her husband—he was a very reserved man, rather gloomy, a man of few words, he scarcely spoke unless spoken
to—I saw them frequently since their marriage—they appeared to life comfortably together, as people in their station generally do—I have seen them at home and in company—they seemed usually on very good terms.
JAMES LINFIELD . I live at No. 8, Tibberton-street, Newington Causeway. I have known the prisoner upwards of six years—I always supposed her of a very humane disposition, very kind, very incapable of such an act as this—I knew her husband—he was a man of very retired and gloomy habits—I had seen them in company together—they appeared to live on very good terms—she always appeared fond of her children.
NOT GUILTY .

3063. JOHN HESKETT was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying Thomas Wellings.
SARAH SHERWOOD . I am the wife of William Sherwood, a wire-worker, of No.5, York-street, London-road. Thomas Wellings was my nephew, and was a wire-worker—on Monday night he was up in my room doing a little work for me—his wife called him down—I went down about ten minutes afterwards, and saw him and Heskett fighting in the middle of York-street—a great many people were standing about—I saw them strike each other—they were perfectly sober—they continued fighting twenty minutes or half an hour—Wellings fell down nearly every time—the prisoner fell down sometimes—they kept on fighting till they fell—there was a number of falls, I cannot say how many—they fell from the blows—Wellings was the last that fell—he fell with the back of his head against the curb-stone—he then said, "I will have no more," and I never heard him speak afterwards—I was not with him when he went home—I went up stairs, and they took him home as well us they could—I was with him when he died—his nose and eye bled—he lived a very little way from me—I sat up with him all night—he never spoke, moved hand or foot, or opened his eyes—I and his wife went for a doctor about eight o'clock—we got Mr. Gale—he came with us—the deceased was about thirty years old—the prisoner had married Wellings' sister—they were on friendly terms before—the prisoner shook hands with him after he fell the last time, when he said he would have no more.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. When he fell the last time did not they both fall together and the deceased undermost? A. They did not fall together—I was standing nearly close to them.
MARY RUSSELL . I lived with Wellings as his wife—he was with Sherwood, and I called him to his tea—he came down—the prisoner was in the street—they had a few words—they began abusing one another and then fought—I do not know which struck first—they both fell—the last time Wellings fell on the curb, and cut his head, and it bled very much—they both had hold of one another and both went down together, Wellings undermost—they were always very good friends—they shook hands first.
BENJAMIN KEMP . I am a chimney-sweeper, and live in York-street—I saw the fight—I was standing at ray door about half-past six o'clock, and heard the prisoner and deceased's wife, as I thought, talking about fighting—the prisoner said he would fight him when he liked—with that the deceased's wife went and called the other man down stairs, and said, "Come down stairs and give him a good hiding" —he came down stairs, and said, he did not know what he was going to fight about, but if the prisoner, wanted to
fight he would fight him—they pulled their jackets off, and fought the first and second rounds—I did not see a blow struck either way—some blows were struck in the course of the fight—when the man was thrown they closed and went down—the prisoner threw the deceased every time—they were both sober—the prisoner was uppermost every time except once—the last round they closed and the prisoner threw him, and the back of his head came against the curb stone—he sat up, and said, he would have no more—the prisoner shook hands with him, and asked if he would have anything to drink, but I believe the deceased never spoke afterwards—he went down the last time from a throw, and not from a blow—I should say there were not more than half a dozen blown in the course of the fight—I should say the deceased struck about oneor two blows—his relations and family were all standing about, but they did not not like to interfere—they might have been picked up once or twice, but I cannot say—there were many people round—the deceased's wife got in before they commenced fighting, but she was away again in a minute or two—she got in between them to prevent their fighting, but some called out for her to come away and let them fight—that was at the commencement of it—she at first called Wellings to come down and fight him, but afterwards she tried to prevent them—she stood by the deceased all the time they were fighting—after they made her come away.
WILLIAM GALE . I am a surgeon—I was called in about eight o'clock, and saw the deceased—he was quite insensible, and in a dying state—I did what I could for him—he had symptoms of effusion of blood in his head from a blow behind the left ear, apparently caused by a fall—that was the cause of his death—there were very trifling bruises about him.
WILLIAM KITCHINGS . I took the prisoner—he said he was very sorry the circumstance had happened.
GUILTY . Aged 25.— Confined Four Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18421024-3064]
3064. GEORGE HUDSON was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of September, 1 metal-cock, value 3 s. 6 d.; 1lb. 6oz. weight of brass, value 1 s.; and 2 metal-plugs, value 2 s.; the goods of our Lady the Queen.—3 other COUNTS, stating them to belong to other persons.
MESSRS. ADOLPHUS and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
ROBERT FRANCIS . I am an Excise-officer. In September, I with another officer, levied on some goods of Messrs. Green, at Battersea. On the 30th of September I delivered the keys to a porter of Mr. Clark's the goods were sold on the premises that day—there was a still and other things—there was a metal tap in one of the casks—I saw the prisoner about—I had information, and spoke to Appleby, the auctioneer's porter—I had seen the prisoner in the room adjoining the room where the oval cask was—I saw a brass tap taken from him—Appleby left him in my charge—I heard something fall—I looked down and found a metal plug close to his heels—Appleby picked it up.
JOHN APPLEBY . I am porter to Mr. Frederick James Clark, an auctioneer, and live in May Fair. On the 30th of September he had a sale at Battersea—Francis delivered the keys to Page, and the whole of the things after that were in my charge—I inspected the property at twelve o'clock—it was all right at that time—after the sale, from information, I looked at
the vat, and found this tap had been pulled out—I went up to the prisoner, who was standing on the grussplot—seeing his pocket hanging down with something heavy, I asked what he had got in his pocket—he replied, "A pair of pincers"—I asked to look at them—he pulled them out—I was satisfied he had something else in it—I asked what it was—he hesitated, and then nodded away from the premises, and said it was a piece of metal he had got over yonder—when he did let me look at it I saw it was a piece of metal belonging to the steam-engine, or something of that kind—I took from his other pocket this metal cock—I left him in custody of Francis, and went to my master—on my return I saw something fall from the prisoner, close to his heels—I picked it up, and it was this plug—I saw the policeman find on him this other plug—when I looked again on the premises, the plugs which had been in the vats, and these other things were missing—the number of things found exactly corresponded with those missing, and they fitted in every respect—the officer missed a piece of metal which had been broken or cutoff—when the prisoner was committed, he said he had done it through distress—he said he had a family at home, but be would not say where.
Prisoner's Defence, The articles were in my possession before I went on the premises.
GUILTY. Aged 48.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury ,— Confined Six Months.

3065. BENJAMIN SMITH and WILLIAM CURLEY were indicted for stealing, on the 27th of September, at Clapham, 5 spoons, value 5 l. 15 s.; 5 forks, value 4 l. 15 s.; 5 sauce ladles, value 2 l. 10 s.; and 2 knife-rests, value 8 s.; the goods of John Milner, in his dwelling-house.
WILLIAM BROON . I am servant to Mr. John Milner, who lives at Clapham Park, in the parish of Clapham—it is his dwelling-house. On the 27th of September, I saw my master's plate safe in the pantry at three o'clock—these five ladles, these knife-rests, and other things ( looking at them) are part of them—I missed them at a quarter after three o'clock—they are worth 13 l. 8 s. 6 d.
WILLIAM JAMES FRANKS . On the 27th of September I saw the two prisoners together coming from Mr. Milner's house.
Smith. He said he could not say whether it was me or not. Witness, I am sure he was one, and they were both together—Smith was against the wall—I spoke to Curley, and told him he was dropping his lucifers, and be took no notice of it—I saw them both come from Mr. Milner's—another lad came from Mr. Shepherd's, and another from another gentleman's premises—they walked a short distance, and then commenced running—I am certain the prisoners are the two persons.
WILLIAM WOODWARD .( police-constable P 157.) I met the prisoners at near four o'clock on the 27th of September—they ran by me—I asked what they had got in their possession—Smith said, "It is all right"—I laid hold of Curley, and of another who got away—I then took Smith, who made great resistance—I took the two prisoners into a shop—on Curley I found these silver articles, and Smith had a box of congreves.
Curley's Defence. The boy who got away told me to carry these things, and said he would pay me for carrying them—I thought he was selling them.
SMITH — GUILTY . Aged 20.— Transported for Ten Years.
CURLEY — GUILTY .* Aged 15.— Transported for Ten Years.—Ship.

3066. CHARLES ALLEN was indicted for feloniously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Thomas Frankcombe, on the 16th of October, at St. George-the-Martyr, Southwark, and stealing therein, 1 gown, value 10 s.; 1 shirt, value 3 s.; 1 shift, value 1 s.; and 1 apron, value 6 d.; the goods of the said Thomas Frankcombe.
JANE FRANKCOMBE . I am the wife of Thomas Frankcombe, and live in Union-place, by the Queen's Bench prison, in the parish of St. George-the-Martyr—it is our dwelling-house. On Sunday evening, the 16th of October I left home about twenty minutes past eight o'clock—I locked the door—I returned in a quarter of an hour, and found the door had been opened, and was bolted inside—I gave an alarm—I looked up stairs, saw a light, and the shadow of a man—I went for a policeman—when I got in I found these things taken from one box, and placed on the lid of another—the policeman burst the door open.
THOMAS FRANKCOMBE . In consequence of information I got on the wall, and saw a man getting over the palings between my house and Cooper's—I ran round, and found the prisoner in custody of Cooper, four doors off—I saw no one but him.
GEORGE COOPER . I heard an alarm of "Stop thief," and in a little time I heard a noise in my back yard, which adjoins Mr. Frank combe's—I went out, and saw the prisoner in my yard—I took hold of him, and said, "You villain, what do you do here?"—he said, "I have done nothing."
Prisoner's Defence. There were two or three of us; we had a drop of drink, and got larking; one of them chucked my hat over the fence, and I got over after it. I got it, and wished to get away, as I heard a cry of "Police."
GUILTY .** Aged 21.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18421024-3067]
3067. JOHN OTZIN, MARY DUDMAN , and ANN CLARK were indicted for stealing, on the 5th of October, 3 shillings, and 4 halfpence, the monies of William Mouls, the master of Otzin.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM JOHN MOULS . I am the son of William Mouls; he keeps the George public-house, in High-street, Newington Butts. On the 5th of October, about half-past nine o'clock in the evening, Dudman and Clark came, and had half a quartern of gin—Otzin, who was our barman for a fortnight, served them—it came to 2 d.—I then saw him take a six-pence—he sounded it on the counter, and put it into the till—he then took. out of the till three shillings and six pennyworth of halfpence, and put it down to Dudman—she put out her hand to take it up—I put my hand on it, and said, "Stop, let me see what you have given there"—I then turned to Joseph Smith, our other barman, and said, "Take notice of what I have taken up here, it is three shillings and a sixpence"—I sent for an officer, and gave Otzin and Dudman into custody—Clark went away, but she went to the station to inquire about Dudman, and the officer brought her to me, to know if she was the other party—I did not observe which of them put down the sixpence—each of them had some of the gin.
JOSEPH SMITH . I am barman to Mr. Mouls. I saw Dudman and Clark in the shop, and Otzin served them with half a quartern of gin—I saw Otzin take a sixpence off the counter, sound it, and put it into the till—he took out some change, and put it on the counter to them—I did not
see at that time what he took out—my young master came forwards, put his hand on it, and took the change up—Dudman had put her hand slightly towards it, but did not take it—my master took up three shillings and six pennyworth of coppers—I went for the officer.
JAMES BRADDICK ( police-sergeant A 30.) I saw Dudman and Clark go to Mr. Mouls' for the first time, on the 27th of September—they both went in—Clark put down a mug, Otzin put a pint of beer into it, and Clark put down 1 s.—I saw Dudman and Clark several times together, between the 27th of September and the 5th of October—I watched them go into Mr. Mouls' eight times one Saturday night, before the 5th of October.
CHARLES BURGESS GOFF ( police-sergeant A 28.) I know Dudman and Clark—I have seen them together—on Saturday, the 1st of October, I saw them go in and out of the prosecutor's house several times—Otzin was in the house, and served them once.
Otzin's Defence. The way I account for the 3 s. 6 d. being on the counter is, Dudman called for half a quartern of gin, and gave me a shilling; and then I understood her to say, "Give me that shilling back, and I will give you half-a-crown." I took that shilling up, and the change. I discovered it, and was going to take it up to rectify it, as Mr. Moul's clapped hit hand on it.
Clark's Defence. I had no knowledge whatever of Otzin. I have been frequently that way, having occasion to meet my husband on the rank at St. George's-road. On several occasions I have met Dudman, and she has asked me to drink, and on this occasion I did so; it was a shilling that was given.
OTZIN — GUILTY . Aged 23.— Transported for Seven Years.
DUDMAN — GUILTY . Aged 30.
CLARK — GUILTY , Aged 24. Confined Six Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
